\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda}(X)\times\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Theta}(Y)\rightarrow\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda\bar{\times}\Theta}(X\times Y).

This is shown in
[
BDH16
, Proposition 5.1, Theorem 6.3, Theorem 6.1 and Theorem 4.6 ]
, with one difference: For their push-forward theorem,
[
BDH16
]
assume that their wavefront set
\Lambda
only contains covectors with basepoint in
C
. This implies the version above, if we can show the following Lemma:

Lemma 2.7.8
.

For
C\subseteq X
closed, we have

\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda}(X)|_{C}=\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda|_{C}}(X)|_{C}

as topological vector spaces.

Proof.

Note that
\Lambda|_{C}=\Lambda\cap(C\times({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\backslash\{0\}))
is the intersection of two closed conic subsets of
\dot{T}^{*}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
and hence closed and conic again.
As
\Lambda|_{C}\subseteq\Lambda
, we automatically have a bounded inclusion

\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda|_{C}}(X)|_{C}\subseteq\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda}(X)|_{C}.

Fix
\eta\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda}(X)|_{C}
. As every element of
\WF(\eta)
has basepoint in

\singsupp(\eta)\subseteq\supp(\eta)\subseteq C,

\eta
has wavefront in
\Lambda|_{C}
, so the two spaces agree as sets. It remains to show that they have the same topology. Consider some seminorm
\|\cdot\|_{k,V,\chi}
of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda|_{C}}(X)|_{C}
. Then
\supp(\chi)\times V
does not intersect
\Lambda|_{C}
and hence
(\supp(\chi)\cap C)\times V
does not intersect
\Lambda
. Let
V_{1}
denote the set of unit vectors in
V
. As
\Lambda
is closed, every

\xi\in(\supp(\chi)\cap C)\times V_{1}

has an open neighborhood
U(\xi)
that is disjoint from
\Lambda
. As product neighborhoods form a basis, we may assume that this is of the form

U(\xi)=U_{1}(\xi)\times U_{2}(\xi).

As
V_{1}
is compact, for each
x\in(\supp(\chi)\cap C)
there are finitely many
(v_{i})_{i\in I}\in V_{1}
such that the corresponding neighborhoods
U_{2}(x,v_{i})
cover
V_{1}
. Set
U^{\prime}(x):=\bigcap\limits_{i\in I}U_{1}(x,v_{i})
. Then
U^{\prime}(x)\times V_{1}
does not intersect
\Lambda
, since any
U^{\prime}(x)\times U_{2}(x,v_{i})
doesn’t. As
(\supp(\chi)\cap C)
is compact, we may find finitely many
(x_{j})_{j\in J}\in(\supp(\chi)\cap C)
such that

O:=\bigcup\limits_{i\in I}U^{\prime}(x_{i})

contains
(\supp(\chi)\cap C)
. By construction
O\times V_{1}
does not intersect
\Lambda
. As
\Lambda
is conic, the same holds for
O\times V
. Choose
\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(O)
that is
1
on
\supp(\chi)\cap C
. Then for arbitrary
\eta\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda}(X)|_{C}
, we have
\psi\chi\eta=\chi\eta
and hence

\|\eta\|_{k,V,\chi}=\|\eta\|_{k,V,\psi\chi}.

Moreover,
\supp(\psi\chi)\times V\subseteq O\times V
does not intersect
\Lambda
, so the right hand side is a seminorm of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda}(X)|_{C}
. Thus the two spaces have the same seminorms and hence the same topology.

∎

2.7.2
Wavefront calculus on manifolds

We now turn from functions on subsets of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
to sections in vector bundles. Defining the wavefront set and extending the previous results for sections can be done in a fairly straightforward way by considering their components in some coordinate chart:

Definition 2.7.9
.

By a ”bundle coordinate” on a vector bundle
F
over a manifold
X
, we shall mean a smooth, fibrewise linear map
F\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}
(these can be canonically identified with sections of
F^{*}
or bundle homomorphisms
F\rightarrow X\times{\mathbb{C}}
over the identity).
Let
A
be a bundle coordinate or a bundle homomorphism over the identity. For a distribution
\eta\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(F)
and any scalar test function
\phi
, define

(A\eta)[\phi]:=\eta[A^{*}\circ\phi],

where
A^{*}
is the fibrewise adjoint of
A
.

We have for any
g\in\Gamma(F)
, test section
\phi
and
\eta\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)
:

A(g\eta)[\phi]=g\eta[A^{*}\circ\phi]=\eta[x\mapsto((A^{*}\phi(x))(g(x)))]=\eta[x\mapsto(\phi(x)(A(g(x))))]=(A\circ g)\eta[\phi].

In particular (set
\eta=1
), we have
Ag=A\circ g
.

With this, we define the wavefront set of a distribution in a vector bundle:

Definition 2.7.10
.

Let
E
be a vector bundle over a manifold
X
.
For
\eta\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(E)
define the wavefront set of
\eta
as the union of all sets of the form

\psi^{*}(\WF((\psi^{-1})^{*}(A\eta|_{\Dom(\psi)})))

for coordinate charts
\psi
and bundle coordinates
A
defined on
\Dom(\psi)
.
The space
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda}(E)
of all distributions with wavefront in a given closed conic set
\Lambda
is topologized by the (strong) seminorms of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(E)
and all seminorms

\|\eta\|_{k,V,\chi,\psi,A}:=\|(\psi^{-1})^{*}(A\eta|_{\Dom(\psi)})\|_{k,V,\chi\circ\psi^{-1}}=\sup\limits_{\xi\in V}(|\xi|+1)^{k}\big|\mathcal{F}\big((\psi^{-1})^{*}(\chi A\eta)\big)(\xi)\big|,

where
\psi
and
A
are as above,
\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Dom(\psi))
is some cut-off,
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
V
is a closed cone in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
such that
\supp(\chi\circ\psi^{-1})\times V
does not intersect
(\psi^{-1})^{*}(\Lambda)
.
Write
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda}(X)
for
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda}(X\times{\mathbb{C}})
.

Basically all the results on subsets of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
generalize to this setting. See the appendix (
Appendix: wavefront calculus on manifolds
) for the proof of the following theorem:

Theorem 2.7.11

(Wavefront calculus on manifolds)
.

The results obtained for scalar functions on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
carry over to sections of vector bundles on manifolds. Assume that
X
and
Y
are manifolds. For
i\in\{1,2,3\}
, let
E_{i}
be a vector bundle over
X
and let
F
be a vector bundle over
Y
. Let
\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}\subseteq\dot{T}^{*}(X)
and
\Theta\subseteq\dot{T}^{*}(Y)
be closed conic subsets.

1.

The seminorms defined for
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda}(E_{1})
are finite. In case
X
is a subset of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and
E_{1}=X\times{\mathbb{C}}
, the new definition coincides with the earlier one.

2.

\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda}(E_{1})
is complete.

3.

\Gamma_{c}(E_{1})
is dense in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda}(E_{1})
.

4.

\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\emptyset}(E_{1})
coincides with
\Gamma(E_{1})
as a topological vector space.

5.

Differential operators between sections of
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
map
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda}(E_{1})
to
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda}(E_{2})
continuously.

6.

Let
f\colon X\rightarrow Y
be a smooth map.

•

If
f^{*}(\Theta)
does not intersect the zero section,
f^{*}
extends to a continuous map from
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Theta}(F)
to
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{f^{*}(\Theta)}(f^{*}(F))
.

•

If
C\subseteq X
is a closed set such that
f|_{C}
is proper,
f_{*}
defines a continuous map from
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda}(f^{*}F)|_{C}
to
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{f_{*}(\Lambda)}(F)
.

•

If
\Lambda\bar{+}\Lambda^{\prime}
does not intersect the zero section, pointwise ”multiplication” of sections in
E_{1}\otimes E_{2}^{*}
and
E_{2}\otimes E_{3}
, defined at each point by

(a\otimes b)\cdot(c\otimes d):=b(c)\cdot(a\otimes d)

extends to a hypocontinuous bilinear map

\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda}(E_{1}\otimes E_{2}^{*})\times\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}(E_{2}\otimes E_{3})\rightarrow\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda\bar{+}\Lambda^{\prime}}(E_{1}\otimes E_{3}).

•

The tensor product defines a hypocontinuous bilinear map

\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda}(E_{1})\times D^{\prime}_{\Theta}(F)\rightarrow\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda\bar{\times}\Theta}(E_{1}\boxtimes F).

•

If
\Lambda\bar{+}\Lambda^{\prime}
does not intersect the zero section and
K\subseteq X
is compact, evaluation of distributions extends to hypocontinuous bilinear maps

\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda}(E_{1})\times\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}(E_{1}^{*})|_{K}\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}

and

\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}(E_{1}^{*})|_{K}\times\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda}(E_{1})\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}.

These are symmetric, in the sense that

\zeta[\eta]=\eta[\zeta].

Remark 2.7.12
.

The multiplication map described here corresponds to pointwise composition when identifying tensor products with spaces of linear maps as in
2.1.1
. Using the other identifications of
2.1.1
, this contains the following as special cases (by setting all non-occuring vector bundles to
X\times{\mathbb{C}}
):

•

Products of scalar functions

\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda}(X)\times\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}(X)\rightarrow\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda\bar{+}\Lambda^{\prime}}(X).

•

Scalar multiplication

\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda}(X)\times\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}(E_{3})\rightarrow\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda\bar{+}\Lambda^{\prime}}(E_{3})

•

Pointwise tensor product

\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda}(E_{1})\times\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}(E_{3})\rightarrow\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda\bar{+}\Lambda^{\prime}}(E_{1}\otimes E_{3}).

•

Pointwise dual pairing

\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda}(E_{2}^{*})\times\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}(E_{2})\rightarrow\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda\bar{+}\Lambda^{\prime}}(X).

The tensor product and pushforward interact in the way one would expect:

Proposition 2.7.13
.

If
f\colon X\rightarrow Y
and
g\colon X^{\prime}\rightarrow Y^{\prime}
are smooth maps between manifolds, we have for distributions
\eta
and
\zeta
in vector bundles over
X
and
X^{\prime}
whose pushforward under
f
respectively
g
is well-defined:

(f_{*}\eta)\otimes(g_{*}\zeta)=(f\times g)_{*}(\eta\otimes\zeta),

where
f\times g(x,x^{\prime}):=(f(x),g(x^{\prime}))
.

Proof.

If
f
and
g
are smooth and
\phi
and
\psi
are test sections, we have

(f_{*}\eta)\otimes(g_{*}\zeta)[\phi\otimes\psi]=\eta[\phi\circ f]\cdot\zeta[\psi\circ g]=\eta\otimes\zeta[(\phi\otimes\psi)\circ(f\times g)]=(f\times g)_{*}(\eta\otimes\zeta)[\phi\otimes\psi].

Thus

(f_{*}\eta)\otimes(g_{*}\zeta)=(f\times g)_{*}(\eta\otimes\zeta).

As smooth functions are dense, this holds for arbitrary distributions.
∎

Chapter 3
Asymptotic expansions related to Green’s operators

In this section, we will develop asymptotic expansions in differentiability orders for the kernels of powers of
G^{\pm}_{P}
and that of
G^{\pm}_{P-z}
for
z\in{\mathbb{C}}
. This is inspired by
[
DW20
]
, where similar results are obtained for Feynman inverses. However, the methods used here are different. While the only things necessary for the proof of our formula for the Hadamard coefficients are the standard Hadamard expansion and the formula for the
z
-dependent Hadamard coefficients shown in Proposition
3.2.4
, the other results might be of independent interest.

3.1
Powers of Green’s operators

We first derive an expansion for powers of the advanced/retarded Green’s operators. Before we can start the main proof, we need to do some technical work to guarantee that the Green’s operators do not decrease differentiability orders too much (locally). This will be necessary to control the remainder terms of our asymptotic expansion. The result is basically a consequence of mapping smooth sections to smooth sections continuously (a better result could be obtained by investigating
G
as a Fourier integral operator).

Proposition 3.1.1
.

Let
A\subset M
be past/future compact compact and
U\subset M
be open and relatively compact. Let
r_{U}
denote restriction to
U
. Then for every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
there is
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
r_{U}\circ G^{\pm}
maps
\Gamma^{m}_{A}(E)
to
\Gamma^{n}(E|_{U})
continuously.

Proof.

We know (see
2.4.9
) that

G^{\pm}\colon\Gamma_{\pm}(E)\rightarrow\Gamma_{\pm}(E)

is continuous. Since the topologies of
\Gamma_{A}(M)
and
\Gamma_{J_{\pm}(A)}(M)
coincide with the subspace topology from
\Gamma_{\pm}
(as the latter carries the limit topology), we know that the restriction

G^{\pm}\colon\Gamma_{A}(E)\rightarrow\Gamma_{J_{\pm}(A)}(E)

is also continuous.
C^{k}
- norms on
{\overline{U}}
are seminorms on the target space. Thus by the seminorm-characterization of continuity, we know that for any
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, there is
c\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
K\subseteq M
such that for all
\phi\in\Gamma_{A}(E)

\|G^{\pm}\phi\|_{C^{n}(\overline{U})}\leq c\|\phi\|_{C^{m}(K)}.

The right hand side is also a seminorm of
\Gamma^{m}_{A}(E)
.
Thus
r_{U}\circ G^{\pm}|_{\Gamma_{A}(E)}
extends continuously to a map
\Gamma^{m}_{A}(E)\rightarrow\Gamma^{n}(E|_{U})
. As
C^{k}
- convergence implies distributional convergence, this continuous extension must coincide with
r_{U}\circ G^{\pm}|_{\Gamma^{m}_{A}(E)}
, as the latter is distributionally continuous.
∎

As Hadamard coefficients and Green’s kernels are sections in
E\boxtimes E^{*}
and we want differentiability in both coordinates, we need to extend the above to the case where
G^{\pm}
acts in the first component on sections of a box product.

Proposition 3.1.2
.

Let
A\subset M
be past/future compact and
U\subset M
be open and relatively compact. Let
O
be some manifold and
F
some vector bundle over
O
. Then for each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, there is
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that for any
f\in\Gamma^{m}_{A\times O}(E\boxtimes F)
, we have
G^{\pm}f|_{U\times O}\in\Gamma^{n}((E\boxtimes F)|_{U\times O})
, where
G^{\pm}f(y,x):=G^{\pm}(f(\cdot,x))(y)
.

Proof.

As differentiability is local, it suffices to show this only on a chart domain in
O
on which
F
is trivial. As a section is smooth if and only if its composition with any bundle coordinate is smooth and the application of an operator in the first component commutes with taking bundle coordinates in the second component, it suffices to consider the case
O={\mathbb{R}}^{j}
,
F={\mathbb{R}}^{j}\times{\mathbb{R}}
. Likewise, we may also assume that
U
is contained in a chart with local trivialization for
E
and thus partial derivatives are well-defined.

For
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, let
m\geq n
be large enough such that for any
k\leq n
,
G^{\pm}
maps
\Gamma^{m-k}_{A}(E)
to
\Gamma^{n-k}(E|_{U})
continuously. This is possible by applying the previous proposition
n
times. We will inductively show the following in the domain of this chart:

Claim
.

For any multiindices
\alpha
and
\beta
with
|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq k\leq n
, we have

\partial_{x}^{\beta}\partial_{y}^{\alpha}G^{\pm}(f(\cdot,x))(y)=\partial_{y}^{\alpha}G^{\pm}(\partial_{x}^{\beta}f(\cdot,x))(y)

with the derivatives on the left hand side existing.

We proceed by induction over
k
, the case
k=0
being immediate. Suppose we have shown the statement for some
k<n
and assume
|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq k
. We have to show for every index
i
:

\partial_{x_{i}}\partial_{y}^{\alpha}G^{\pm}(\partial_{x}^{\beta}f(\cdot,x))(y)=\partial_{y}^{\alpha}G^{\pm}(\partial_{x_{i}}\partial_{x}^{\beta}f(\cdot,x))(y)

(including existence of the derivative).
We have for
h>0
(
e_{i}
denoting the
i
-th standard unit vector) and any compact set
K
:

\displaystyle\|\frac{\partial_{x}^{\beta}f(\cdot,x+he_{i})-\partial_{x}^{\beta}f(\cdot,x)}{h}-\partial_{x_{i}}\partial_{x}^{\beta}f(\cdot,x)\|_{C^{m-|\beta|-1}(K)}

\displaystyle=\|\frac{1}{h}\int\limits_{0}^{h}\partial_{x_{i}}\partial_{x}^{\beta}f(\cdot,x+te_{i})-\partial_{x_{i}}\partial_{x}^{\beta}f(\cdot,x)dt\|_{C^{m-|\beta|-1}(K)}

\displaystyle\leq\sup\limits_{t<h}\|\partial_{x_{i}}\partial_{x}^{\beta}f(\cdot,x+te_{i})-\partial_{x_{i}}\partial_{x}^{\beta}f(\cdot,x)\|_{C^{m-|\beta|-1}(K)}.

All derivatives of
\partial_{x_{i}}\partial_{x}^{\beta}f(y,x)
of order up to
m-|\beta|-1
are continuous and hence uniformly continuous on the compact set

K\times\{x+te_{i}\mid t\in[0,1]\}.

Thus the end result of the above equation converges to
0
for
h\rightarrow 0
. This means that the differential quotient

\frac{\partial_{x}^{\beta}f(\cdot,x+he_{i})-\partial_{x}^{\beta}f(\cdot,x)}{h}

converges to
\partial_{x_{i}}\partial_{x}^{\beta}f(\cdot,x)
in
\Gamma^{m-|\beta|-1}_{A}(E)
. As
G^{\pm}
maps
C^{m-|\beta|-1}
-sections to
C^{n-|\beta|-1}
sections continuously and
\partial_{y}^{\alpha}
maps those to
C^{0}
-sections (over
U\times O
) continuously, we have

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{i}}\partial_{y}^{\alpha}G^{\pm}(\partial_{x}^{\beta}f(\cdot,x))(y)

\displaystyle=\lim\limits_{h\rightarrow 0}\partial_{y}^{\alpha}G^{\pm}\left(\frac{\partial_{x}^{\beta}f(\cdot,x+he_{i})-\partial_{x}^{\beta}f(\cdot,x)}{h}\right)(y)

\displaystyle=\partial_{y}^{\alpha}G^{\pm}\left(\lim\limits_{h\rightarrow 0}\frac{\partial_{x}^{\beta}f(\cdot,x+he_{i})-\partial_{x}^{\beta}f(\cdot,x)}{h}\right)(y)

\displaystyle=\partial_{y}^{\alpha}G^{\pm}(\partial_{x_{i}}\partial_{x}^{\beta}f(\cdot,x))(y),

which finishes the proof of the claim.

To conclude the proof of the proposition, note that an arbitrary partial derivative has the form

\prod\limits_{j=0}^{l}\partial_{x}^{\beta_{j}}\partial_{y}^{\alpha_{j}}

and iterated application of the claim yields that

\prod\limits_{j=0}^{l}\partial_{x}^{\beta_{j}}\partial_{y}^{\alpha_{j}}G^{\pm}(f(\cdot,x))(y)=\prod\limits_{j=0}^{l}\partial_{y}^{\alpha_{j}}G^{\pm}(\prod\limits_{j=0}^{l}\partial_{x}^{\beta_{j}}f(\cdot,x))(y)

exists and is continuous.
∎

We now introduce the precise form of the objects for which we want an asymptotic expansion. Morally, they can be thought of as a rigorous version of

\mathcal{K}(G^{\pm m})(\cdot,x).

Definition 3.1.3
.

For
x\in M
,
m\in\mathbb{Z}
define distributions in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(E\otimes E_{x}^{*})
via

G^{\pm m}_{x}:=(G^{\pm})^{m}\delta_{x}

(where
\delta_{x}
is the Dirac distribution at
x
, defined by
\delta_{x}[\psi\otimes v]:=\psi(x)(v)
for
\psi\in\Gamma_{c}(E)
and
v\in E^{*}_{x}
).
In case
m=1
, we omit it:

G^{\pm}_{x}:=G^{\pm 1}_{x}.

Remark 3.1.4
.

For negative
m
, we obtain Schwartz kernels for powers of
P
(”evaluated” at
x
) as both
G^{+}
and
G^{-}
are an inverse for
P
on a suitable space.

Note that here we used the notation introduced at the end of
2.1.1
, i.e.
G^{\pm}
acts as the identity in the
E^{*}_{x}
-part. There are multiple other ways to think of
G^{\pm m}_{x}
. As we will show later, it is the pullback of
\mathcal{K}(G)
with respect to the inclusion of
M
at
x
(see Lemma
6.1.5
). Another way to see it is evaluation at
x
composed with the adjoint of
(G^{\pm})^{m}
:

Proposition 3.1.5
.

For any
\psi\in\Gamma_{c}(E^{*})
, we have

G^{\pm m}_{x}[\psi]=(G^{\mp}_{P^{*}})^{m}\psi(x)

Proof.

Let
f_{n}
be a sequence of test functions converging distributionally to
\delta_{x}
. Then we have by Proposition
2.4.14
and continuity of
G^{\pm}
:

\displaystyle G^{\pm m}_{x}(\psi)

\displaystyle=(G^{\pm})^{m}\delta_{x}(\psi)

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\int\limits_{M}\psi((G^{\pm})^{m}f_{n})

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\int\limits_{M}(G^{\mp}_{P^{*}})^{m}\psi(f_{n})

\displaystyle=\delta_{x}((G^{\mp}_{P^{*}})^{m}\psi)

\displaystyle=(G^{\mp}_{P^{*}})^{m}\psi(x).\qed

To obtain the asymptotic expansion we want, we first need to restrict to a suitable subset of
M
. Such subsets will be reffered to as GE sets (it is left to the reader to decide whether this stands for ”Green’s expansion” or for ”good enough”).

Definition 3.1.6
.

A subset
U
of
M
shall be called GE, if it is open, geodesically convex, globally hyperbolic, and causally compatible.

Remark 3.1.7
.

Openness is required to restrict distributions to
U
. Geodesic convexity ensures the existence of Riesz distributions and of Hadamard coefficients. Global hyperbolicity ensures that
U
has unique Green’s operators, while causal compatibility ensures that those agree with the restriction of the Green’s operators on
M
.

We shall make use of the following fact:

Proposition 3.1.8
.

Every
p\in M
has a basis of GE neighborhoods.

Proof.

This is basically the statement of
[
Min14
, Corollary 2 and Remark 14]
, which asserts the existence of a neighborhood basis of geodesically convex, globally hyperbolic, causally convex sets. Causally convex means that for any neighborhood
U
from this basis, its intersection with every causal curve is connected. We only need to show that this implies causal compatibility, which is not too difficult: If
y\in J_{\pm}^{M}(x)
for
x\in U
, there must be a future/past directed causal curve
\gamma
in
M
that connects
x
and
y
. As the intersection of
\gamma
with
U
is connected and contains
x
and
y
, it must also contain the segment between
x
and
y
, so the two can be joined by a future/past directed causal curve in
U
. Thus
x\in J_{\pm}^{U}(y)
and hence
U
is causally compatible.
∎

We can now prove the main theorem of this section

Theorem 3.1.9
.

Let
U\subseteq M
be a GE set and let
m\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then we have the asymptotic expansion

G^{\pm m}_{x}|_{U}\sim_{x}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\binom{m+k-1}{k}V^{k,U}_{x}R^{U}_{\pm}(2k+2m,x).

For
m<0
, the right hand side is a finite sum, as all summands with
k+m>0
vanish, and we have equality.

Remark 3.1.10
.

Note that the special case
m=1
reproduces the expansion of
[
BGP07
, Proposition 2.5.1]
, though on slightly different domains (this means that even though they are morally the same, it is not easy to reduce either one to the other). Unlike in
[
BGP07
]
, there is no assumption on the volume of
U
and it may be chosen independently of
P
.

Remark 3.1.11
.

In case
m<0
, this is actually a formula for the kernel of powers of
P
. All even Riesz distributions of order
0
or less are the same in the advanced and retarded case and supported only at the diagonal, so this expansion is not as surprising as it might seem on first glance. From the case
m=-1
, we obtain that

\mathcal{K}(P)(\cdot,x)=V^{0}_{x}R(-2,x)-V^{1}_{x}\delta_{x}.

In particular,
P
is uniquely determined by the metric
g
(which determines the Riesz-distributions) and the first two Hadamard coefficients
V^{0}
and
V^{1}
.

Proof.

We proceed by two-way induction on
m
, showing that if the statement holds for
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, it also holds for
m+1
if
m\geq 0
and for
m-1
, if
m\leq 0
. The case
m=0
follows from
R_{\pm}(0,x)=\delta_{x}
. Fix
x\in U
.

We make some preliminary calculations. For
k+m\neq 0
we can use the characterization of
\rho_{x}^{U}
(
2.5.9
) and the transport equations (see
2.5.10
) to calculate

\displaystyle\binom{k+m}{k}P(V^{k,U}_{x}R^{U}_{\pm}(2k+2m+2,x))

\displaystyle=\binom{k+m}{k}\Big(\frac{-1}{2k+2m}(\rho_{x}^{U}-2k-2m)V^{k,U}_{x}R^{U}_{\pm}(2k+2m,x)+(PV^{k,U}_{x})R^{U}_{\pm}(2k+2m+2,x)\Big)

\displaystyle=\binom{k+m}{k}\Big(\frac{-1}{2k+2m}((2kPV^{k-1,U}_{x}-2mV^{k,U}_{x})R^{U}_{\pm}(2k+2m,x))

\displaystyle+(PV^{k,U}_{x})R^{U}_{\pm}(2k+2m+2,x)\Big)

\displaystyle=\binom{k+m}{k}\frac{2m}{2k+2m}V^{k,U}_{x}R^{U}_{\pm}(2k+2m,x)-\binom{k+m}{k}\frac{2k}{2m+2k}(PV^{k-1,U}_{x})R^{U}_{\pm}(2k+2m,x)

\displaystyle+\binom{k+m}{k}(PV^{k,U}_{x})R^{U}_{\pm}(2k+2m+2,x)

\displaystyle=\binom{k+m-1}{k}V^{k,U}_{x}R^{U}_{\pm}(2k+2m,x)-\binom{k+m-1}{k-1}(PV^{k-1,U}_{x})R^{U}_{\pm}(2k+2m,x)

\displaystyle+\binom{k+m}{k}(PV^{k,U}_{x})R^{U}_{\pm}(2k+2m+2,x))

In the special case
k=m=0
, we obtain:

\displaystyle P(V^{0,U}_{x}R_{\pm}(2,x))

\displaystyle=\lim\limits_{\alpha\rightarrow 0}P(V^{0,U}_{x}R_{\pm}(\alpha+2,x))

\displaystyle=\lim\limits_{\alpha\rightarrow 0}P(V^{0,U}_{x})R_{\pm}(\alpha+2,x)-(\frac{1}{\alpha}\rho_{x}^{U}V^{0,U}_{x}-V^{0,U}_{x})R_{\pm}(\alpha,x)

\displaystyle=\lim\limits_{\alpha\rightarrow 0}P(V^{0,U}_{x})R_{\pm}(\alpha+2,x)+V^{0,U}_{x}R_{\pm}(\alpha,x)

\displaystyle=P(V^{0,U}_{x})R_{\pm}(2,x)+V^{0,U}_{x}R_{\pm}(0,x)

\displaystyle=P(V^{0,U}_{x})R_{\pm}(2,x)+V^{0,U}_{x}(x)\delta_{x}

\displaystyle=\delta_{x}+P(V^{0,U}_{x})R_{\pm}(2,x).

either way, we obtain for
k=0
:

P(V^{0,U}_{x}R^{U}_{\pm}(2m+2,x))=V^{0,U}_{x}R^{U}_{\pm}(2m,x)+(PV^{0,U}_{x})R^{U}_{\pm}(2m+2,x)

Putting everything together, for any
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
in case
m\geq 0
and for any
N<-m
if
m<0
, we have

\displaystyle P\sum\limits_{k=0}^{N}\binom{m+k}{k}V^{k,U}_{x}R^{U}_{\pm}(2k+2m+2,x)

\displaystyle=V^{0,U}_{x}R^{U}_{\pm}(2m,x)+(PV^{0,U}_{x})R^{U}_{\pm}(2m+2,x)+\sum\limits_{k=1}^{N}\binom{k+m-1}{k}V^{k,U}_{x}R^{U}_{\pm}(2k+2m,x)

\displaystyle-\binom{k+m-1}{k-1}(PV^{k-1,U}_{x})R^{U}_{\pm}(2k+2m,x)+\binom{k+m}{k}(PV^{k,U}_{x})R^{U}_{\pm}(2k+2m+2,x)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{N}\binom{k+m-1}{k}V^{k,U}_{x}R^{U}_{\pm}(2k+2m,x)-\sum\limits_{k=0}^{N-1}\binom{k+m}{k}(PV^{k,U}_{x})R^{U}_{\pm}(2k+2m+2,x)

\displaystyle+\sum\limits_{k=0}^{N}\binom{k+m}{k}(PV^{k,U}_{x})R^{U}_{\pm}(2k+2m+2,x))

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{N}\binom{k+m-1}{k}V^{k,U}_{x}R^{U}_{\pm}(2k+2m,x)+\binom{N+m}{N}(PV^{N,U}_{x})R^{U}_{\pm}(2N+2m+2,x)

\displaystyle=:\sum\limits_{k=0}^{N}\binom{k+m-1}{k}V^{k,U}_{x}R^{U}_{\pm}(2k+2m,x)+E_{N}(\cdot,x)

where

E_{N}(\cdot,x):=\binom{N+m}{N}(PV^{N,U}_{x})R^{U}_{\pm}(2N+2m+2,x)

is
C^{n}
in both variables for
N\geq\frac{d}{2}+n-m
.

Case 1:
m+1\Rightarrow m
,
m<0

Suppose for induction that

G^{\pm m+1}_{x}=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{-m-1}\binom{m+k}{k}V^{k,U}_{x}R^{U}_{\pm}(2k+2m+2,x)

(This implies the theorem for
m+1
, as all further summands are 0). Let
N=-m-1
.
Using the fact (see
[
BGP07
, below Proposition 2.3.1]
that

PV^{N,U}_{x}(x)=-V^{N+1,U}_{x}(x)

and the identity

\binom{-a}{b}=(-1)^{b}\binom{a+b-1}{b}

for integer
b
, we calculate

\displaystyle E_{N}(\cdot,x)

\displaystyle=\binom{-1}{N}(PV^{N,U}_{x})R^{U}_{\pm}(0,x)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{N}\binom{N}{N}(PV^{N,U}_{x})\delta_{x}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{N}PV^{N,U}_{x}(x)\delta_{x}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{N+1}V^{N+1,U}_{x}(x)\delta_{x}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{N+1}\binom{N+1}{N+1}V^{N+1,U}_{x}(x)\delta_{x}

\displaystyle=\binom{-1}{N+1}V^{N+1,U}_{x}R^{U}_{\pm}(0,x)

\displaystyle=\binom{-1}{-m}V_{x}^{-m,U}R_{\pm}^{U}(0,x).

We obtain from the inductive hypothesis and our previous calculations:

\displaystyle G^{\pm m}_{x}

\displaystyle=PG^{\pm m+1}_{x}

\displaystyle=P\sum\limits_{k=0}^{N}\binom{m+k}{k}V^{k,U}_{x}R^{U}_{\pm}(2k+2m+2,x)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{N}\binom{k+m-1}{k}V^{k,U}_{x}R^{U}_{\pm}(2k+2m,x)+E_{N}(\cdot,x)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{-m-1}\binom{k+m-1}{k}V^{k,U}_{x}R^{U}_{\pm}(2k+2m,x)+\binom{-1}{-m}V^{-m,U}_{x}R^{U}_{\pm}(0,x)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{-m}\binom{k+m-1}{k}V^{k,U}_{x}R^{U}_{\pm}(2k+2m,x)

Case 2:
m\Rightarrow m+1
,
m\geq 0

Fix
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and assume the theorem holds for some
m\geq 0
. We first show that the expansion holds on relatively compact GE subsets of
U
. Let
W,O\subseteq U
be relatively compact (in
U
) and GE. Proposition
3.1.2
implies that there is
n^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
G^{\pm}_{P|_{U}}
maps
\Gamma^{n^{\prime}}_{J^{U}_{\pm}(\overline{O})\times O}(E|_{U}\boxtimes E^{*}|_{U})
to sections that are
C^{n}
on
W\times O
. Assume
x\in O
. By the inductive hypothesis, we may choose
N\geq\frac{d}{2}+n^{\prime}
such that

F_{N}(\cdot,x):=G^{\pm m}_{x}|_{U}-\sum\limits_{k=0}^{N}\binom{m+k-1}{k}V^{k,U}_{x}R^{U}_{\pm}(2k+2m,x)

defines a
C^{n^{\prime}}
-section
F_{N}
on
U
. We then have

\displaystyle G^{\pm m+1}_{x}|_{U}

\displaystyle=(G^{\pm}_{P}G^{\pm m}_{x})|_{U}

\displaystyle=G^{\pm}_{P|_{U}}G^{\pm m}_{x}|_{U}

\displaystyle=G^{\pm}_{P|_{U}}\left(\sum\limits_{k=0}^{N}\binom{m+k-1}{k}V^{k,U}_{x}R^{U}_{\pm}(2k+2m,x)+F_{N}(\cdot,x)\right)

\displaystyle=G^{\pm}_{P|_{U}}\left(P\sum\limits_{k=0}^{N}\binom{m+k}{k}V^{k,U}_{x}R^{U}_{\pm}(2k+2m+2,x)-E_{N}(\cdot,x)+F_{N}(\cdot,x)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{N}\binom{m+k}{k}V^{k,U}_{x}R^{U}_{\pm}(2k+2m+2,x)+G^{\pm}_{P|_{U}}(F_{N}(\cdot,x)-E_{N}(\cdot,x))

\displaystyle=:\sum\limits_{k=0}^{N}\binom{m+k}{k}V^{k,U}_{x}R^{U}_{\pm}(2k+2m+2,x)+\tilde{F}_{N}(\cdot,x).

with

\tilde{F}_{N}(\cdot,x):=G^{\pm m+1}_{x}|_{U}-\sum\limits_{k=0}^{N}\binom{m+k}{k}V^{k,U}_{x}R^{U}_{\pm}(2k+2m+2,x)=G^{\pm}_{P|_{U}}(F_{N}(\cdot,x)-E_{N}(\cdot,x)).

By our choice of
{n^{\prime}}
and
N
,
\tilde{F}_{N}
is
C^{n}
on
W\times O
, as both
E_{N}|_{U\times O}
and
F_{N}|_{U\times O}
are
C^{n^{\prime}}
and supported in
J_{\pm}(O)\times O
. Note that the only choices we made after choosing
W
and
O
(apart from assuming
x\in O
) were that of
{n^{\prime}}
and
N
. We thus want to make the expansion independent of the choice of
N
.

Let
N_{0}\geq\frac{d}{2}+n
be arbitrary. For
k\geq N_{0}
, all Riesz distributions
R^{U}_{\pm}(2k+2m+2,x)
are given by
C^{n}
-functions. We see that

\displaystyle\tilde{F}_{N_{0}}(\cdot,x)

\displaystyle=G^{\pm m+1}_{x}|_{U}-\sum\limits_{k=0}^{N_{0}}\binom{m+k}{k}V^{k,U}_{x}R^{U}_{\pm}(2k+2m+2,x)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k=N_{0}+1}^{N}\binom{m+k}{k}V^{k,U}_{x}R^{U}_{\pm}(2k+2m+2,x)+\tilde{F}_{N}(\cdot,x)

is
C^{n}
on
W\times O
. As this is independent of
W
and
O
, these can replaced by arbitrary relatively compact GE sets. All points in
U
have relatively compact GE neighborhoods (choose any relatively compact neighborhood and then choose a GE neighborhood contained in it by Proposition
3.1.8
). Thus any
(y,x)\in U\times U
has a neighborhood on which
\tilde{F}_{N_{0}}
is
C^{n}
, which means it is
C^{n}
on all of
U\times U
. As
n
was arbitrary, we have the desired asymptotic expansion for
m+1
and have thus concluded our induction.
∎

3.2
Green’s “resolvent”

We now consider the Green’s operators for the operator
P-z
for
z\in{\mathbb{C}}
, which is still normally hyperbolic. If we view
G^{\pm}_{P}
as something like an inverse for
P
, then
G^{\pm}_{P-z}
is something like a resolvent. We seek to derive an expansion similar to the one above, where only the Hadamard coefficients of
P
rather than
P-z
appear and the
z
-dependence is instead shifted to a generalzed version of Riesz distributions. For this section, fix a convex open set
U
and let
p\in U
be some point. Any Hadamard coefficients, Riesz distributions and similar objects depending on a convex open subset will be defined on
U
unless otherwise indicated and we will omit the superscript
U
.

Definition 3.2.1
.

Let
\square_{V}
denote the d’Alembertian on a Lorentzian vector space
V
.

For
z\in{\mathbb{C}}
and
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we define resolvent Riesz distributions as

R_{\pm}(z,2m,x):=(\exp_{x})^{-1*}(G^{\pm m}_{\square_{V}-z,0})

The motivation for this is that it reproduces the original Riesz-distributions in the case
z=0
:

Proposition 3.2.2
.

On a Lorentzian vector space
V
, we have

R^{V}_{\pm}(2m)=G^{\pm m}_{\square_{V},0}.

Thus we have

R_{\pm}(0,2m,x)=R_{\pm}(2m,x).

Proof.

We have (
[
BGP07
, Lemma 1.2.2]
)

\square_{V}R_{\pm}^{V}(2m+2)=R_{\pm}^{V}(2m)

and hence

R_{\pm}^{V}(2m+2)=G^{\pm}_{\square_{V}}\square_{V}R_{\pm}^{V}(2m+2)=G^{\pm}_{\square_{V}}R_{\pm}^{V}(2m).

Together with

R_{\pm}^{V}(0)=\delta_{0},

we find that the
R_{\pm}^{V}(2m)
satisfy the recursion defining
G^{\pm m}_{\square_{V},0}
, hence we have shown the first part.

The second part follows by inserting the first part into the definition of the Riesz distributions on
U
and comparing with that of the resolvent Riesz distributions.
∎

We want to exploit the asymptotic expansion that we already have in order to obtain the one we would like to get. For this, we need to know the Hadamard coefficients for
P-z
in terms of those for
P
.

Definition 3.2.3
.

For
z\in{\mathbb{C}}
, let
V^{k}_{x}(z)
be the corresponding Hadamard coefficients for
P-z
.

Proposition 3.2.4
.

We have

V^{k}_{x}(z)=\sum\limits_{m=0}^{k}\binom{k}{m}z^{m}V^{k-m}_{x}.

Remark 3.2.5
.

The corresponding formula for the heat coefficients

a_{k}(\Delta-z)=\sum\limits_{m=0}^{k}\frac{1}{m}z^{m}a_{k-m}(\Delta)

can be shown analogously. Alternatively, that formula can also be deduced by Taylor expanding
e^{tz}
and multiplying out the expansions in

e^{-t(\Delta-z)}=e^{tz}e^{-t\Delta}.

Proof.

Let

V_{k}(z):=\sum\limits_{m=0}^{k}\binom{k}{m}z^{m}V^{k-m}_{x}

We need to show that
V_{0}(z)(x,x)=1
and the
V_{k}(z)
satisfy the transport equations.
The former holds, as
V_{0}(z)=V^{0}_{x}
. For the latter, we calculate using the transport equation for
P
:

\displaystyle(\rho_{x}^{U}-2k)V_{k}(z)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{m=0}^{k}\binom{k}{m}z^{m}(\rho_{x}^{U}-2k)V^{k-m}_{x}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{m=0}^{k}\binom{k}{m}z^{m}((\rho_{x}^{U}-2(k-m))V^{k-m}_{x}-2mV^{k-m}_{x})

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{m=0}^{k}\binom{k}{m}z^{m}2(k-m)PV^{k-m-1}_{x}-\sum\limits_{m=0}^{k}\binom{k}{m}z^{m}2mV^{k-m}_{x}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{m=0}^{k}\binom{k-1}{m}z^{m}2kPV^{k-m-1}_{x}-\sum\limits_{m=1}^{k}\binom{k-1}{m-1}z^{m}2kV^{k-m}_{x}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{m=0}^{k-1}\binom{k-1}{m}z^{m}2kPV^{k-m-1}_{x}-\sum\limits_{m=0}^{k-1}\binom{k-1}{m}z^{m+1}2kV^{k-m-1}_{x}

\displaystyle=2k(P-z)\sum\limits_{m=0}^{k-1}\binom{k-1}{m}z^{m}V^{k-m-1}_{x}

\displaystyle=2k(P-z)V_{k-1}(z).

The
V_{k}(z)
thus satisfy the transport equations for
P-z
, so they are the desired Hadamard coefficients.
∎

The other ingredient we need is an expansion for the resolvent Riesz distributions in terms of the standard ones.

Lemma 3.2.6
.

On Minkowski space, we have the asymptotic expansion

G^{\pm k}_{\square-z,0}\sim\sum\limits_{m=0}^{\infty}\binom{k+m-1}{m}z^{m}R_{\pm}(2m+2k).

Proof.

As
R_{\pm}(2m+2k)=R^{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}_{\pm}(2m+2k,0)
, Theorem
3.1.9
applied to
\square-z
gives us the asymptotic expansion

G^{\pm k}_{\square-z,0}\sim\sum\limits_{m=0}^{\infty}\binom{k+m-1}{m}V_{m}(z)R_{\pm}(2m+2k),

where the
V_{m}(z)
are the Hadamard coefficients at
0
for
\square-z
, i.e. the unique solutions to

V_{0}(z)(0)=1

and

\rho_{0}^{{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}}V_{m}(z)-2mV_{m}(z)=2m(\square-z)V_{m-1}(z).

These equations are solved by the constant functions
V_{m}(z):=z^{m}
, as

\rho_{0}^{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}z^{m}=\left<\grad\gamma,\grad z^{m}\right>+(\square\gamma-d)z^{m}=0

and

\square z^{m}=0,

so we obtain

G^{\pm k}_{\square-z,0}\sim\sum\limits_{m=0}^{\infty}\binom{k+m-1}{m}z^{m}R_{\pm}(2m+2k).\qed

This carries over from Minkowski space to
M
in a straightforward way:

Lemma 3.2.7
.

We have the asymptotic expansion

R_{\pm}(z,2k,x)\sim_{x}\sum\limits_{m=0}^{\infty}\binom{k+m-1}{m}z^{m}R^{U}_{\pm}(2m+2k,x).

Proof.

As
U
is convex, it is contractible and thus
TU
is trivial. Let
\phi\colon U\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\rightarrow TU
be an isometric trivialization that preserves time-orientation.
As
\phi
is a time-orientation preserving isometry on every fibre, it preserves all objects defined only in terms of the metric and time-orientation, i.e.

\phi_{x}^{-1*}(R^{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}_{\pm}(2m+2k))=R^{T_{x}M}_{\pm}(2m+2k)

and

\phi_{x}^{-1*}(G^{\pm k}_{\square_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}-z,0})=G^{\pm k}_{\square_{T_{x}M}-z,0}

Let

\Exp\colon\Dom(\Exp)\subseteq TU\rightarrow U\times U

denote the exponential map with variable basepoint, i.e.
\Exp(v)=(x,\exp_{x}(v))
for
v\in T_{x}U
. This is smooth.

Let
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
be arbitrary. For
N
large enough such that

G^{\pm k}_{\square_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}-z,0}=\sum\limits_{m=0}^{N}\binom{k+m-1}{m}z^{m}R^{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}_{\pm}(2m+2k)+F

with
F\in C^{n}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
,
we have

\displaystyle R_{\pm}(z,2k,x)

\displaystyle=(\exp_{x})^{-1*}(G^{\pm k}_{\square_{T_{x}M}-z,0})

\displaystyle=(\exp_{x}\circ\phi_{x})^{-1*}(G^{\pm k}_{\square_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}-z,0})

\displaystyle=(\exp_{x}\circ\phi_{x})^{-1*}\left(\sum\limits_{m=0}^{N}\binom{k+m-1}{m}z^{m}R^{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}_{\pm}(2m+2k)+F\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{m=0}^{N}\binom{k+m-1}{m}z^{m}(exp_{x})^{-1*}R^{T_{x}M}_{\pm}(2m+2k)+F\circ\phi_{x}^{-1}\circ exp_{x}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{m=0}^{N}\binom{k+m-1}{m}z^{m}R^{U}_{\pm}(2m+2k,x)+F\circ\phi^{-1}\circ\Exp^{-1}(\cdot,x).

as
F\circ\phi^{-1}\circ\Exp^{-1}
is
C^{n}
and
n
was arbitrary, we have the desired expansion.
∎

We now have everything in place to prove the main result of this section: If, in the asymptotic expansions for the Green’s operators of
P
, we replace the standard Riesz distributions with the resolvent Riesz distributions for
z\in{\mathbb{C}}
, then we obtain asymptotic expansions for the Green’s operators of
P-z
.

Theorem 3.2.8
.

If
U\subseteq M
is GE, we have the following asymptotic expansion in
U
:

G^{\pm}_{P-z,x}|_{U}\sim_{x}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}V^{k,U}_{x}R^{U}_{\pm}(z,2k+2,x).

More generally,

G^{\pm m}_{P-z,x}|_{U}\sim_{x}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\binom{k+m-1}{k}V^{k,U}_{x}R^{U}_{\pm}(z,2k+2m,x).

Proof.

Applying Theorem
3.1.9
to
P-z
and inserting our formula for the
z
-dependent Hadamard coefficients (Proposition
3.2.4
), we obtain

\displaystyle G^{\pm m}_{P-z,x}|_{U}

\displaystyle\sim_{x}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\binom{k+m-1}{k}V^{k}_{x}(z)R_{\pm}(2k+2m,x)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\binom{k+m-1}{k}\sum\limits_{l+n=k}\binom{k}{n}z^{n}V^{l}_{x}R_{\pm}(2k+2m,x)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{l=0}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}\binom{n+l+m-1}{n+l}\binom{n+l}{n}z^{n}V^{l}_{x}R_{\pm}(2(n+m+l),x)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{l=0}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}\binom{n+l+m-1}{n}\binom{l+m-1}{l}z^{n}V^{l}_{x}R_{\pm}(2(n+m+l),x)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{l=0}^{\infty}\binom{l+m-1}{l}V^{l}_{x}\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}\binom{n+l+m-1}{n}z^{n}R_{\pm}(2(n+m+l),x)

\displaystyle\sim_{x}\sum\limits_{l=0}^{\infty}\binom{l+m-1}{l}V^{l}_{x}R_{\pm}(z,2(m+l),x).

Here we used the identity

\binom{a}{b}\binom{b}{c}=\binom{a}{c}\binom{a-c}{b-c}

and Lemma
3.2.7
.

This proves the second claim, the first claim follows as the special case
m=1
.
∎

Chapter 4
Constructing an action-like function

In this chapter we will take our first step towards extracting the Hadamard coefficients from the asymptotic expansion we have seen. We will construct a function from the Green’s operator that has an asymptotic expansion in terms of the Hadamard coefficients. This is similar to an analogue of a spectral action, but with additional terms coming from the Hadamard coefficients’ derivatives.

For technical reasons, we’ll be looking not at the two Green’s operators individually, but at their difference, sometimes referred to as the causal propagator.

Definition 4.0.1
.

Set

G:=G^{+}-G^{-},

G_{x}:=G^{+}_{x}-G^{-}_{x},

R(\alpha):=R_{+}(\alpha)-R_{-}(\alpha)

and

R^{U}(\alpha,x):=R^{U}_{+}(\alpha,x)-R^{U}_{-}(\alpha,x).

Taking the difference of the asymptotic expansions for
G^{\pm}_{x}
, we obtain

G_{x}|_{U}\sim_{x}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}V^{k,U}_{x}R^{U}(2k+2,x).

We are motivated by the following formal calculation:

Let
w
be a timelike geodesic in
M
and let
x=w(0)
. Then we have (for
\Re(\alpha)
large enough)

R^{U}(\alpha,x)(w(t))=c_{\alpha}|t|^{\alpha-d}\sign(t)

Writing

\tilde{V}_{k}(t):=V^{k,U}_{x}(w(t)),

we can taylor expand

\tilde{V}_{k}(t)\sim\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\tilde{V}_{k}^{(n)}(0)t^{n}.

For some function
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})
and
s>0
, we can then calculate (formally):

\displaystyle\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}G_{x}(w(t))f(\tfrac{t}{s})dt

\displaystyle\sim\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}V^{k,U}_{x}(w(t))R^{U}(2k+2,x)(w(t))f(\tfrac{t}{s})dt

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}\tilde{V}_{k}(t)c_{2k+2}t^{2k+2-d}\sign(t)f(\tfrac{t}{s})dt

\displaystyle\sim\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}c_{2k+2}\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\tilde{V}_{k}^{(n)}(0)t^{2k+n+2-d}\sign(t)f(\tfrac{t}{s})dt

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{c_{2k+2}}{n!}\tilde{V}_{k}^{(n)}(0)s^{2k+n+3-d}\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{s}\left(\frac{t}{s}\right)^{2k+n+2-d}\sign(\tfrac{t}{s})f(\tfrac{t}{s})dt

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{c_{2k+2}}{n!}\tilde{V}_{k}^{(n)}(0)s^{2k+n+3-d}\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}t^{2k+n+2-d}\sign(t)f({t})dt

\displaystyle=:\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{k,n}(f)\tilde{V}_{k}^{(n)}(0)s^{2k+n+3-d}

with some coefficients
a_{k,n}(f)
independent of
s
,
M
and
P
(except through the dimension). From this, or something similar, one might hope to extract the values of the Hadamard coefficients at the diagonal, i.e.
\tilde{V}_{k}(0)
. The problem of performing this extraction is postponed to the next chapter, as this chapter is devoted to making the above calculation precise. The main problem in this is that, for small
k
, the Riesz-distributions are not given by functions, so evaluation at
w(t)
is ill-defined.

4.1
Holomorphicity of Riesz distributions for some wavefront

While evaluating the Riesz-distributions at a single point is problematic, we will show that restricting them to a timelike geodesic is possible. By wavefront calculus, it suffices to show that the Riesz-distributions’ wavefront does not intersect the conormal bundle of the curve. To actually compute what the restriction does, we also need that the Riesz-distributions are holomorphic in the space of distributions with a suitable wavefront, so that the restriction is still holomorphic. Showing these two things is the goal of this section.

The conormal bundle of a timelike geodesic consists of spacelike vectors. Therefore we want to show that the wavefront set of the Riesz distributions does not contain spacelike vectors. This is the point where we have to use that we work with the difference
R=R_{+}-R_{-}
, rather than the advanced or retarded Riesz distributions individually. In this difference, the worst singularities at
0
will cancel out. As wavefront calculus is invariant under diffeomorphisms, it will be sufficient to work on Minkowski space.

We thus consider a spacelike vector
\xi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
. Deciding whether
\xi
is in the wavefront set of a function involves integration against
e^{-i\left<\xi,\cdot\right>}
. Our strategy is to find, for each
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, another point
O_{\xi}(x)
such that

e^{-i\left<\xi,O_{\xi}(x)\right>}=e^{i\left<\xi,x\right>}

and such that the value of
R_{+}(\alpha)
at
x
cancels with that of
R_{-}(\alpha)
at
O_{\xi}(x)
. Thus the integral of
R(\alpha)
over each level set of
e^{-i\left<\xi,\cdot\right>}
vanishes (
O_{\xi}
will also be volume preserving). We will construct
O_{\xi}
on each level set as a reflection through (multiples of) a suitable vector
y_{\xi}
.
y_{\xi}
and
O_{\xi}
will be constructed in the next two theorems and are illustrated in the following graphic.

\xi
x+\xi^{\bot}
x
O_{\xi}(x)
\left<\xi,x\right>y_{\xi}
x^{\prime}
O_{\xi}(x^{\prime})
x^{\prime\prime}
O_{\xi}(x^{\prime\prime})

Definition/Proposition 4.1.1
.

For any spacelike unit vector
\xi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, there is a spacelike
y_{\xi}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
such that
\left<y_{\xi},\xi\right>=1
and for all
v\bot\xi
and
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we have

\gamma(\lambda y_{\xi}+v)=\gamma(\lambda y_{\xi}-v).

(scalar product and orthogonality refer to the euclidean scalar product on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.)

Remark 4.1.2
.

On each
\xi
-orthogonal hyperplane
\left<\cdot,\xi\right>=\lambda
,
\gamma
is given by a (higher-dimensional) parabola.
\lambda y_{\xi}
is the minimum of this parabola, around which it is symmetric.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we may assume that

\xi=\cos(\theta)e_{0}+\sin(\theta)e_{1},

where
e_{i}
is the
i
-th standard unit vector and

\theta:=\arccos(\left<\xi,e_{0}\right>)\in(\frac{\pi}{4},\frac{3\pi}{4}),

as the problem is symmetric under rotation in the spatial coordinates (the condition on
\theta
is equivalent to
|\xi_{0}|<|\xi_{1}|
). Let
R
be the rotation in the first two coordinates that maps
e_{0}
to
\xi
, i.e.

R={\begin{pmatrix}\cos(\theta)&-\sin(\theta)\\
\sin(\theta)&\cos(\theta)\end{pmatrix}}\oplus 1.

R
to maps planes orthogonal to
\xi
to planes where
x_{0}
is constant. There the problem basically reduces to finding the minimum of a quadratic function in
x_{1}
:
Writing
x_{r}
to denote the last
d-2
components of
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, we have

\displaystyle\gamma(Rx)

\displaystyle=\gamma(\cos(\theta)x_{0}-\sin(\theta)x_{1},\sin(\theta)x_{0}+\cos(\theta)x_{1},x_{r})

\displaystyle=(\cos(\theta)x_{0}-\sin(\theta)x_{1})^{2}-(\sin(\theta)x_{0}+\cos(\theta)x_{1})^{2}-\|x_{r}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=(\cos(\theta)^{2}-\sin(\theta)^{2})(x_{0}^{2}-x_{1}^{2})-4\sin(\theta)\cos(\theta)x_{0}x_{1}-\|x_{r}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\cos(2\theta)(x_{0}^{2}-x_{1}^{2})-2\sin(2\theta)x_{0}x_{1}-\|x_{r}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=-\cos(2\theta)((x_{1}+\tan(2\theta)x_{0})^{2}-(1+\tan(2\theta)^{2})x_{0}^{2})-\|x_{r}\|^{2}.

On the plane
x_{0}=\lambda
, this takes its minimum at
\lambda e_{0}-\lambda\tan(2\theta)e_{1}
, which we will thus take as our point for reflection.
We have for
w\bot e_{0}
and
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}
:

\displaystyle\gamma(R(\lambda e_{0}-\lambda\tan(2\theta)e_{1}+w))

\displaystyle=-\cos(2\theta)((w_{1})^{2}-(1+\tan(2\theta)^{2})\lambda^{2})-\|w_{r}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=-\cos(2\theta)((-w_{1})^{2}-(1+\tan(2\theta)^{2})\lambda^{2})-\|-w_{r}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\gamma(R(\lambda e_{0}-\lambda\tan(2\theta)e_{1}-w)).

To solve our original problem on the plane
\left<x,\xi\right>=\lambda
, we simply rotate back.
Define
y_{\xi}:=R(e_{0}-\tan(2\theta)e_{1})
. If
v\bot\xi
, then
R^{-1}v\bot e_{0}
, so we have

\gamma(\lambda y_{\xi}+v)=\gamma(R(\lambda e_{0}-\lambda\tan(2\theta)e_{1}+R^{-1}v))=\gamma(R(\lambda e_{0}-\lambda\tan(2\theta)e_{1}-R^{-1}v))=\gamma(\lambda y_{\xi}-v)

as desired. It remains to show that
y_{\xi}
is indeed spacelike. We can reuse the calculation above to obtain

\displaystyle\gamma(y_{\xi})

\displaystyle=\gamma(R(1e_{0}-\tan(2\theta)e_{1}))

\displaystyle=-\cos(2\theta)(0-(1+\tan(2\theta)^{2})1)-0

\displaystyle=\cos(2\theta)\left(\frac{\cos(2\theta)^{2}+\sin(2\theta)^{2}}{\cos(2\theta)^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\cos(2\theta)}.

As
2\theta\in(\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{3\pi}{2})
, this is negative, hence
y_{\xi}
is spacelike.
∎

We can now define
O_{\xi}
by reflecting through
y_{\xi}
in each
\xi
-orthogonal hyperplane:

Definition/Proposition 4.1.3
.

For each spacelike
\xi
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, the linear map
O_{\xi}
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
defined by

O_{\xi}(x):=2\left<x,\xi\right>y_{\xi}-x

satisfies:

1.

For any
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, there are
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
v\bot\xi
such that
x=\lambda y_{\xi}+v
and
O_{\xi}(x)=\lambda y_{\xi}-v

2.

O_{\xi}
leaves hyperplanes orthogonal to
\xi
invariant, i.e. for all
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
:

\left<x,\xi\right>=\left<O_{\xi}(x),\xi\right>

3.

O_{\xi}
is a Lorentz-transformation, i.e.

\gamma\circ O_{\xi}=\gamma

4.

O_{\xi}
preserves volume, i.e.

|det(O_{\xi})|=1

5.

O_{\xi}
maps future oriented vectors to past oriented vectors, i.e.

\sign(O_{\xi}(x)_{0})=-\sign(x_{0})

for any causal vector
x
.

Proof.

1.

y_{\xi}
is not in
\xi^{\bot}
and hence

\spann(\xi^{\bot}\cup\{y_{\xi}\})={\mathbb{R}}^{d}.

Thus any
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
can be written as in Part
1
.
As

O_{\xi}(\lambda y_{\xi}+v)=2\left<\lambda y_{\xi},\xi\right>y_{\xi}-(\lambda y_{\xi}+v)=\lambda y_{\xi}-v,

we get Part
1
.
We also get Part
2
, as

\left<\lambda y_{\xi}+v,\xi\right>=\left<\lambda y_{\xi},\xi\right>=\left<\lambda y_{\xi}-v,\xi\right>.

Part
3
immediately follows from Part
1
and Lemma
4.1.1
.
The more customary definition of a Lorentz transform, that

\eta(O_{\xi}(x),O_{\xi}(y))=\eta(x,y),

where
\eta
is the Minkowski metric, follows from Part
3
and the polarization identity

-\eta(x,y)=\frac{1}{2}(\gamma(x+y)-\gamma(x)-\gamma(y)).

Part
4
is true for any Lorentz transformation and follows form applying determinants to the matrix version of the Lorentz condition

O_{\xi}^{T}\eta O_{\xi}=1.

Finally, note that

\frac{1}{2}(x+O_{\xi}(x))=\left<x,\xi\right>y_{\xi}

is spacelike and in the convex hull of
x
and
O_{\xi}(x)
. Lorentz transformations map causal vectors to causal vectors and the past and future solid lightcone are each convex. Thus, for any causal vector
x
,
x
and
O_{\xi}(x)
must be contained in different lightcones, otherwise their convex hull could not contain a spacelike vector. This proves Part
5
.∎

Definition 4.1.4
.

For
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
define
\sigma(x):=sign(x_{0})
.

We will consider general functions of the form

\sigma\gamma^{*}(f)(x)=\sigma(x)f(\gamma(x))

for functions
f
on
{\mathbb{R}}
with support in
[0,\infty)
and singular support at
0
. This includes
R(\alpha)
for
\Re(\alpha)
large enough as the special case
f(x)=c_{\alpha}\mathbbm{1}_{x\geq 0}x^{\frac{\alpha-d}{2}}
. We want to show that no spacelike vector
\xi
is in the wavefront of
\sigma\gamma^{*}f
.

Morally, what we want to do is to use the transformation formula and invariance (up to sign) of the integrand under
O_{\xi}
to compute (ignoring integrability concerns)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(\sigma\gamma^{*}f)(\xi)

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}e^{-i\left<x,\xi\right>}\sigma(x)f(\gamma(x))dx

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}e^{-i\left<O_{\xi}(x),\xi\right>}\sigma(O_{\xi}(x))f(\gamma(O_{\xi}(x)))dx

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}e^{-i\left<x,\xi\right>}(-\sigma(x))f(\gamma(x))dxdz

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{F}(\sigma\gamma^{*}f)(\xi).

and conclude that
\mathcal{F}(\sigma\gamma^{*}f)(\xi)
is 0 and hence rapidly decaying. However, we need to multiply the integrand with arbitrary cutoff-functions to obtain wavefront norms of
\sigma\gamma^{*}(f)
. These will not be invariant under
O_{\xi}
, so we cannot immediately apply the reasoning above. The strategy is to first introduce a suitable family of
\xi
-invariant cutoff-functions and then use those to show the statement for arbitrary cutoffs.

Lemma 4.1.5
.

Let
V
be a closed cone of spacelike vectors. Then there is a family of
\psi_{\xi}\in C^{\infty}_{c}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
for
{\xi\in V\cap S^{d-1}}
satisfying the following:

1.

\psi_{\xi}\circ O_{\xi}=\psi_{\xi}

2.

all
\psi_{\xi}
are constantly 1 on the unit ball
B_{1}(0)

3.

there is a compact set
K
such that all
\psi_{\xi}
are supported in
K
.

4.

all
\|\psi_{\xi}\|_{C^{k}}
are bounded uniformly in
\xi
.

Proof.

Let
\theta
again denote the angle between
\xi
and
e_{0}
. As
V\cap S^{d-1}
is compact,
\tan(2\theta)
is bounded on
V
by some constant
C-1
. Then
y_{\xi}
, as defined in
4.1.1
has norm at most
C
for
\xi
in
V
. Choose
\chi\in C^{\infty}_{c}({\mathbb{R}})
that is 1 on
[-1,1]
and
0
outside
[-2,2]
. Choose
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}})
such that
\phi(x)
is
1
for
x\leq C+1
and
0
for
x\geq C+2
. Define

\psi_{\xi}(x):=\chi(\left<x,\xi\right>)\phi(\|x-\left<x,\xi\right>y_{\xi}\|).

For
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
v\in\xi^{\bot}
and
x=\lambda y_{\xi}+v
, we have
\left<x,y_{\xi}\right>=\lambda
and hence

\psi_{\xi}(x)=\chi(\lambda)\phi(\|\lambda y_{\xi}+v-\lambda y_{\xi}\|)=\chi(\lambda)\phi(\|v\|)=\chi(\lambda)\phi(\|-v\|)=\psi_{\xi}(\lambda y_{\xi}-v)=\psi_{\xi}(O_{\xi}(x)).

Thus
1
. holds.

For
x\in B_{1}(0)
, we have

|\left<x,\xi\right>|\leq 1

and

\|x-\left<x,\xi\right>y_{\xi}\|\leq\|x\|+\|y_{\xi}\|\leq C+1.

Thus
\psi_{\xi}
is 1 on the unit ball and hence
2
. holds.

Now assume
x\in supp(\psi_{\xi})
. Then we have

|\left<x,\xi\right>|\leq 2

and

\|x-\left<x,\xi\right>y_{\xi}\|\leq C+2.

Thus

\|x\|\leq C+2+2\|y_{\xi}\|\leq 3C+2

and we have
\supp(\psi_{\xi})\subseteq K:=B_{3C+2}(0)
, so
3
. holds.

As
(\xi,x)\mapsto\partial^{\alpha}\psi_{\xi}(x)
is continuous, it is bounded on the compact set
(V\cap S^{d-1})\times K
. Thus we obtain
4
. as well.
∎

With these
\psi_{\xi}
, we can now make the moral calculation from before rigorous:

Proposition 4.1.6
.

For any
f\in C^{0}({\mathbb{R}})
supported in
{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}
,
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
\xi\in V\cup S^{d-1}
, we have

\mathcal{F}(\psi_{\xi}\sigma\gamma^{*}f)(\lambda\xi)=0

Proof.

Using first the transformation formula and then invariance under
O_{\xi}
up to sign of
\left<\cdot,\xi\right>
,
\psi_{\xi}
,
\sigma
(on causal vectors) and
\gamma
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(\psi_{\xi}\sigma\gamma^{*}f)(\lambda\xi)

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}e^{-i\lambda\left<x,\xi\right>}\psi_{\xi}(x)\sigma(x)f(\gamma(x))dx

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}e^{-i\lambda\left<O_{\xi}(x),\xi\right>}\psi_{\xi}(O_{\xi}(x))\sigma(O_{\xi}(x))f(\gamma(O_{\xi}(x)))dx

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}e^{-i\lambda\left<x,\xi\right>}\psi_{\xi}(x)(-\sigma(x))f(\gamma(x))dx

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{F}(\psi_{\xi}\sigma\gamma^{*}f)(\lambda\xi).\qed

We will now need the notations and results of wavefront calculus described in Section
2.7
.
In order to generalize estimates from
\psi_{\xi}
to more general cutoff functions, we will have to delve into more technical considerations on wavefront norms.

Proposition 4.1.7
.

For any
u\in L^{1}_{loc}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, closed cones
V,V^{\prime}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\backslash\{0\}
such that
V
contains an open neighborhood of
V^{\prime}
and functions
\psi,\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
we have

\|\chi u\|_{n,V^{\prime},\psi}\leq C\|\chi\|_{C^{n}}(\|u\|_{n,V,\psi}+\|\psi u\|_{L^{1}})

with some constant
C
depending on
V
,
V^{\prime}
and
m
.

Proof.

Let
v:=\psi u
.
Choose
c
such that for
k\in V^{\prime}
with
|k|=1
, we have
B_{c}(k)\subset V
. By scaling invariance, we have for arbitrary
k\in V^{\prime}
that
B_{c|k|}(k)\subseteq V
.
We have

\displaystyle(|k|+1)^{n}\mathcal{F}(\chi v)(k)

\displaystyle=(|k|+1)^{n}(\mathcal{F}(\chi)*\mathcal{F}(v))(k)

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{|\xi|<c|k|}(|k|+1)^{n}\hat{\chi}(\xi)\hat{v}(k-\xi)d\xi+\int\limits_{|\xi|\geq c|k|}(|k|+1)^{n}\hat{\chi}(\xi)\hat{v}(k-\xi)d\xi

As for arbitrary
\xi
, we have

1+|k|\leq 1+|k-\xi|+|\xi|\leq 1+|k-\xi|+|\xi|+|\xi||k-\xi|=(|k-\xi|+1)(|\xi|+1),

we can estimate the first summand by

\displaystyle|\int\limits_{|\xi|<c|k|}(|k|+1)^{n}\hat{\chi}(\xi)\hat{v}(k-\xi)d\xi|

\displaystyle\leq|\int\limits_{|\xi|<c|k|}(|\xi|+1)^{n}\hat{\chi}(\xi)(|k-\xi|+1)^{n}\hat{v}(k-\xi)d\xi|

\displaystyle\leq\|(|\cdot|+1)^{n}\hat{\chi}\|_{L^{1}}\sup\limits_{k^{\prime}\in V}((|k^{\prime}|+1)^{n}|\hat{v}(k^{\prime})|)

\displaystyle\leq C\|\chi\|_{C^{n}}\|u\|_{n,V,\psi}

For the second summand, we use that

|\hat{v}(k-\xi)|\leq\|v\|_{L^{1}}.

Thus we get

\displaystyle|\int\limits_{|\xi|\geq c|k|}(|k|+1)^{n}\hat{\chi}(\xi)\hat{v}(k-\xi)d\xi|

\displaystyle\leq|\int\limits_{|\xi|\geq c|k|}(|c^{-1}\xi|+1)^{n}\hat{\chi}(\xi)\|v\|_{L^{1}}d\xi|

\displaystyle\leq|\int\limits_{|\xi|\geq c|k|}(|c^{-1}\xi|+1)^{n}\hat{\chi}(\xi)\|v\|_{L^{1}}d\xi|

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime}\|(|\cdot|+1)^{n}\hat{\chi}\|_{L^{1}}\|v\|_{L^{1}}

\displaystyle\leq C\|\chi\|_{C^{n}}\|v\|_{L^{1}}.

Overall, we can conclude

\|\chi u\|_{n,V^{\prime},\psi}=\sup\limits_{k\in V^{\prime}}(|k|+1)^{n}\mathcal{F}(\chi v)(k)\leq C\|\chi\|_{C^{n}}(\|u\|_{n,V,\psi}+\|v\|_{L^{1}}).\qed

We want to show that the Riesz distributions are holomorphic in the space of distributions with some suitable wavefront
\Lambda
. As the Riesz distributions are the image of the functions
c_{\alpha}x^{\alpha}\mathbbm{1}_{(0,\infty)}
under the operator
\sigma\gamma^{*}
, we want to show that this operator is continuous between suitable spaces. The domain to consider should be large enough to contain the above functions (for some values of
\alpha
) but no larger than necessary, so that it is as easy as possible to show continuity of
\sigma\gamma^{*}
on this domain. This motivates the following definition:

Definition 4.1.8
.

Let
C^{+}_{\infty}
denote the space of all continuous functions on
{\mathbb{R}}
that vanish on
(-\infty,0]
and are smooth on
(0,\infty)
, endowed with both the seminorms of
C^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}\backslash\{0\})
(i.e. the
C^{k}(K)
-norms for compact
K
with
0\notin K
) and the seminorms of
C_{0}({\mathbb{R}})
(supremum norms on compact sets).

Define for the remainder of this chapter:

\Lambda:=\{(x,v)\in\dot{T}^{*}_{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})\mid\text{$x$ lightlike and $v$ parallel to $d\gamma(x)$ or $x=0$ and $v$ not spacelike}\}.

We can now show the technical centerpiece of this section:

Theorem 4.1.9
.

The map
\sigma\gamma^{*}
defined by
\sigma\gamma^{*}(f)=\sigma f\circ\gamma
is a continuous map from
C^{+}_{\infty}
to
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
.

Proof.

As

\sup\limits_{x\in K}|\sigma f(\gamma(x))|=\sup\limits_{y\in\gamma(K)}|f(y)|

and
\gamma(K)
is compact for
K
compact,
\sigma\gamma^{*}
is continuous as a map into
L^{\infty}_{loc}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
. This embeds continuously in the space of distributions, so
\sigma\gamma^{*}
maps continuously into
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
(with respect to the strong topology). Thus we only need to check that
\sigma\gamma^{*}
is bounded with respect to the wavefront-set related norms

\|u\|_{n,V,\psi}

for
\supp(\psi)\times V\cap\Lambda=\emptyset
.

Wavefront norms away from 0
:

First assume that
0\notin\supp(\phi)
. Then we may as well exclude
0
from the domain of
\sigma f\circ\gamma
when calculating the corresponding norms. But on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\backslash\{0\}
,
\gamma
is a submersion, which allows us to use wavefront calculus: As
d\gamma
is non-vanishing, we find that

\gamma|_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\backslash\{0\}}^{*}(T_{0}({\mathbb{R}}))=\{(x,v)\in\dot{T}^{*}_{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}\backslash\{0\})\mid\gamma(x)=0,v=cd\gamma(x)\text{ for $c\in{\mathbb{R}}\backslash\{0\}$}\}

does not intersect the zero section, so
\gamma|_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\backslash\{0\}}^{*}
maps continuously from
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{T^{*}_{0}({\mathbb{R}})}({\mathbb{R}})
into
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\gamma|_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\backslash\{0\}}^{*}(T_{0}({\mathbb{R}}))}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}\backslash\{0\})=\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda|_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\backslash\{0\}}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}\backslash\{0\})
.

The seminorms of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}({\mathbb{R}})
are bounded by those of
C^{0}({\mathbb{R}})
. The remaining seminorms of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{T_{0}({\mathbb{R}})}({\mathbb{R}})
, i.e. the seminorms
\|\cdot\|_{n,V,\psi}
for
0\notin\supp(\psi)
are also seminorms for
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\emptyset}({\mathbb{R}}\backslash\{0\})=C^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}\backslash\{0\})
. Thus all seminorms of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{T^{*}_{0}({\mathbb{R}})}({\mathbb{R}})
are bounded by those of
C^{+}_{\infty}
, so we can conclude that
\gamma|_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\backslash\{0\}}^{*}
also maps
C^{+}_{\infty}
to
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda|_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\backslash\{0\}}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}\backslash\{0\})
continuously.

Choose a partition of unity
(\chi_{1},\chi_{2},\chi_{3})
subordinate to the open cover

\left(\{x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\mid x_{0}>0\},\{x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\mid x_{0}<0\},{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\backslash\gamma^{-1}({\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}})\right)

of
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\backslash\{0\}
. Then
\tilde{\sigma}:=\chi_{1}-\chi_{2}
is a smooth function on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\backslash\{0\}
that coincides with
\sigma
on
\gamma^{-1}({\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}})\backslash\{0\}
. Thus on
C^{+}_{\infty}
, we have

\sigma\gamma|_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\backslash\{0\}}^{*}=\tilde{\sigma}\gamma|_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\backslash\{0\}}^{*}

and this maps continuously from
C^{+}_{\infty}
to
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda|_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\backslash\{0\}}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}\backslash\{0\})
, since multiplication with smooth functions is continuous on the latter space. We can conclude that all seminorms
\|\sigma\gamma^{*}(f)\|_{n,V,\psi}

with
0\notin\supp(\psi)
and
\supp(\psi)\times V\cap\Lambda=\emptyset
are bounded by the
C^{+}_{\infty}
seminorms of
f
.

Wavefront norms at 0
:

It now remains to bound the norms with
0\in\supp(\psi)
. The condition
\supp(\psi)\times V\cap\Lambda=\emptyset
is then equivalent to
V
containing only spacelike vectors. Thus we now fix
\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
and a closed cone
V
of spacelike vectors in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
. For spacelike vectors Proposition
4.1.6
almost gives us the estimates we want, only with
\psi_{\xi}
instead of
\psi
. Thus what we need to do now is replace these special
\xi
-dependent test functions with arbitrary ones. We will first replace the
\psi_{\xi}
with a fixed cutoff
\phi
(indepedent of
\xi
), which in turn will be replaced with the arbitrary cutoff
\psi
.

We will successively have to make our cones smaller in the following estimate, so we need to choose slightly bigger cones. Let
V^{\prime}
be a closed spacelike cone whose interior contains
V
and let
V^{\prime\prime}
be a closed spacelike cone whose interior contains
V^{\prime}
.
Use the notation of Lemma
4.1.5
(i.e. construct
\psi_{\xi}
) for
V^{\prime\prime}
instead of
V
.
Choose
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
that is one on the unit ball
B_{1}(0)
and supported in
K
(the compact set from
4.1.5
). Let
\chi
be a function in
C_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
that is 1 on
(K\cup\supp(\psi))\backslash B_{1}(0)
and vanishes around 0.

For any
f\in C^{+}_{\infty}
we have:

\displaystyle\ \|\sigma\gamma^{*}f\|_{n,V^{\prime},\phi}

\displaystyle=\sup\limits_{\xi\in V^{\prime},\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}(|\lambda|+1)^{n}\mathcal{F}(\phi\sigma\gamma^{*}f)(\lambda\xi)

\displaystyle=\sup\limits_{\xi\in V^{\prime},\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}(|\lambda|+1)^{n}\mathcal{F}((\phi-\psi_{\xi})\sigma\gamma^{*}f)(\lambda\xi)

\displaystyle\leq\sup\limits_{\xi,k\in V^{\prime},\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}(|\lambda|+1)^{n}\mathcal{F}((\phi-\psi_{\xi})\sigma\gamma^{*}f)(\lambda k)

where in the second step we have subtracted
0
by Proposition
4.1.6
. Using that

\chi(\phi-\psi_{\xi})=(\phi-\psi_{\xi})

and then Proposition
4.1.7
, we can then estimate (with constants independent of
f
):

\displaystyle\ \|\sigma\gamma^{*}f\|_{n,V^{\prime},\phi}

\displaystyle\leq\sup\limits_{\xi,k\in V^{\prime},\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}(|\lambda|+1)^{n}\mathcal{F}(\chi(\phi-\psi_{\xi})\sigma\gamma^{*}f)(\lambda k)

\displaystyle=\sup\limits_{\xi\in V^{\prime}}\|(\phi-\psi_{\xi})(\sigma\gamma^{*}f)\|_{n,V^{\prime},\chi}

\displaystyle\leq C\sup\limits_{\xi\in V^{\prime}}\|\phi-\psi_{\xi}\|_{C^{n}}(\|\sigma\gamma^{*}f\|_{n,V^{\prime\prime},\chi}+\|\chi\sigma\gamma^{*}f\|_{L^{1}})

\displaystyle\leq C(\|\phi\|_{C^{n}}+\sup\limits_{\xi\in V^{\prime}}\|\psi_{\xi}\|_{C^{n}})(\|\sigma\gamma^{*}f\|_{n,V^{\prime\prime},\chi}+\|\chi\sigma\gamma^{*}f\|_{L^{1}})

\displaystyle=C^{\prime}(\|\sigma\gamma^{*}f\|_{n,V^{\prime\prime},\chi}+\|\chi\sigma\gamma^{*}f\|_{L^{1}})

As
0\notin\supp(\chi)
, the first summand is one of the seminorms we have already estimated.
The second summand can be estimated by

\|\chi\sigma\gamma^{*}f\|_{L^{1}}\leq\|\chi\|_{L^{1}}\|\sigma\gamma^{*}f\|_{L^{\infty}(\supp(\chi))}\leq\|\chi\|_{L^{1}}\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\gamma(\supp(\chi)))}

and is thus bounded by
C^{+}_{\infty}
seminorms of
f
.

Define
\chi^{c}:=\mathbbm{1}_{B_{1}(0)}(1-\chi)
. We have

\psi=\chi^{c}\psi+\chi\psi

and

\phi\chi^{c}\psi=\chi^{c}\psi

Using Proposition
4.1.7
again, we can estimate:

\displaystyle\|\sigma\gamma^{*}f\|_{n,V,\psi}

\displaystyle\leq\|\chi^{c}\psi\sigma\gamma^{*}f\|_{n,V,\phi}+\|\sigma\gamma^{*}f\|_{n,V,\chi\psi}

\displaystyle\leq C(\|\sigma\gamma^{*}f\|_{n,V^{\prime},\phi}+\|\phi\sigma\gamma^{*}f)\|_{L^{1}})+\|\sigma\gamma^{*}f\|_{n,V,\chi\psi},

The first summand we already estimated, for the second we obtain as for the second summand above

\|\phi(\sigma\gamma^{*}f)\|_{L^{1}}\leq\|\phi\|_{L^{1}}\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\gamma(\supp(\phi)))}.

Overall, we obtain

\|\sigma\gamma^{*}f\|_{n,V,\psi}\leq C(\|\sigma\gamma^{*}f\|_{n,V^{\prime\prime},\chi}+\|\sigma\gamma^{*}f\|_{n,V,\chi\psi}+\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\gamma(\supp(\chi)))}+\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\gamma(\supp(\phi)))}),

for some constant
C
independent of
f
. The first two summands are wavefront norms away from zero, which are bounded by
C^{+}_{\infty}
-seminorms by the first part of this proof. The other two summands are already
C^{+}_{\infty}
-seminorms. Thus we have bounded all seminorms of
\sigma\gamma^{*}f
in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
in terms of the seminorms of
f
in
C^{+}_{\infty}
. We can conclude that
\sigma\gamma^{*}
is a continuous map between those spaces. ∎

To show the properties we want about the Riesz distributions, we now need to study those functions in
C^{+}_{\infty}
that get mapped to Riesz distributions by
\sigma\gamma^{*}
.

Definition/Proposition 4.1.10
.

The family of functions

X_{\alpha}(x):=\mathbbm{1}_{(0,\infty)}(x)x^{\alpha}

is holomorphic on
\Re(\alpha)>0
as a map into
C^{+}_{\infty}
(any undefined powers of
x
are set to
0
but are irrelevant anyways due to the characteristic function).

Proof.

The obvious candidate for a derivative is given by the pointwise derivative:

\partial_{\alpha}X_{\alpha}:=\mathbbm{1}_{(0,\infty)}(x)ln(x)x^{\alpha}.

This is still in
C^{+}_{\infty}
, as
ln(x)
grows slower than
x^{-\alpha}
for
x\rightarrow 0
. We have for non-zero
h\in{\mathbb{C}}
such that
|h|<\Re(\alpha)
:

\displaystyle E_{h}(x)

\displaystyle:=\frac{X_{\alpha+h}(x)-X_{\alpha}(x)}{h}-\partial_{\alpha}X_{\alpha}(x)

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{0}^{1}\partial_{\alpha}X_{\alpha+sh}(x)-\partial_{\alpha}X_{\alpha}(x)ds

\displaystyle=\partial_{\alpha}X_{\alpha}(x)\int\limits_{0}^{1}(x^{sh}-1)ds.

We need to show that
E_{h}
converges to
0
in all
C^{+}_{\infty}
-seminorms for
h\rightarrow 0
. Let
\epsilon\in(0,1)
be arbitrary.
Let
K\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}
be compact and
x\in K
be positive (everything is
0
for
x\leq 0
). If
x\in(0,\epsilon)
, we can estimate
|x^{sh}-1|<1
to obtain

|\partial_{\alpha}X_{\alpha}(x)\int\limits_{0}^{1}(x^{sh}-1)ds|\leq\sup\limits_{x^{\prime}\in(0,\epsilon)}|\partial_{\alpha}X_{\alpha}(x^{\prime})|.

For
x\geq\epsilon
, we get

|\partial_{\alpha}X_{\alpha}(x)\int\limits_{0}^{1}(x^{sh}-1)ds|\leq\sup\limits_{x^{\prime}\in K}|\partial_{\alpha}X_{\alpha}(x^{\prime})|\sup\limits_{y\in[\epsilon,K]}|y^{h}-1|\leq C\max\{\max(K)^{h}-1,1-\epsilon^{h}\}.

Thus we obtain overall

\displaystyle\limsup\limits_{h\rightarrow 0}\|E_{h}\|_{L^{\infty}(K)}

\displaystyle\leq\limsup\limits_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\limsup\limits_{h\rightarrow 0}\max\{\sup\limits_{x\in(0,\epsilon)}|\partial_{\alpha}X_{\alpha}(x)|,C(\max(K)^{h}-1),C(1-\epsilon^{h})\}

\displaystyle=\limsup\limits_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\max\{\sup\limits_{x\in(0,\epsilon)}|\partial_{\alpha}X_{\alpha}(x)|,0,0\}

\displaystyle=\limsup\limits_{x\rightarrow 0}|\partial_{\alpha}X_{\alpha}(x)|

\displaystyle=0.

To show convegence of the
C^{\infty}((0,\infty))
-norms of
E_{h}
, it suffices to show that

\int\limits_{0}^{1}(x^{sh}-1)ds

converges to zero in these seminorms, as multiplication with a smooth function is bounded in all
C^{k}
-norms. Thus we compute for
|h|<1
,
k\geq 1
,
K\subseteq(0,\infty)
compact and
x\in K
:

\displaystyle|\partial_{x}^{k}\int\limits_{0}^{1}(x^{sh}-1)ds|

\displaystyle=|\int\limits_{0}^{1}\prod\limits_{j=0}^{k-1}(sh-j)x^{sh-k}ds|

\displaystyle\leq|h|\prod\limits_{j=1}^{k-1}(j+|h|)\int\limits_{0}^{1}\max\{|x|^{-1-k},|x|^{1-k}\}ds

\displaystyle\leq k!\max\{\min(K)^{-1-k},\min(K)^{1-k}\}|h|.

We can conclude that
E_{h}
converges to 0 in all
C^{k}(K)
-seminorm. As we have already shown uniform convergence on
{\mathbb{R}}
, this means that the differential quotient converges to
\partial_{\alpha}X_{\alpha}
in all seminorms of
C^{+}_{\infty}
, so
X_{\alpha}
is holomorphic in that space.
∎

The main result of this section is now easy to prove, using the above and the extension technique also employed in
[
BGP07
]
:

Proposition 4.1.11
.

The family
R(\alpha)=R_{+}(\alpha)-R_{-}(\alpha)
is holomorphic (as a function of
\alpha
) in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda}
(for
\Lambda
as in definition
4.1.8
).

Proof.

For
\Re(\alpha)>d
, we have

R(\alpha)(x)=c_{\alpha}\gamma(x)^{\frac{\alpha-d}{2}}\sigma(x)\mathbbm{1}_{\gamma(x)>0}=c_{\alpha}\sigma\gamma^{*}(X_{\frac{\alpha-d}{2}})(x).

X_{\frac{\alpha-d}{2}}
is holomorphic in
C^{+}_{\infty}
for
\Re(\alpha)>d
and
\sigma\gamma^{*}
maps this continuously into
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda}
, so
R(\alpha)
is holomorphic in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda}
for
\Re(\alpha)>d
. Let
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
be arbitrary. As
\square
maps
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda}
to itself continuously, we can conclude that

R(\alpha-2k)=\square^{k}R(\alpha)

is holomorphic in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda}
for
\Re(\alpha)>d
. Thus
R(\alpha)
is holomorphic in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda}
for
\Re(\alpha)>d-2k
. As
k
was arbitrary, it is holomorphic on all of
{\mathbb{C}}
.
∎

4.2
Integrating along a timeline

We now seek to integrate Riesz distributions and Green’s kernels along a timelike curve
w\colon I\rightarrow M
, as outlined in the beginning of this section. Evaluating the Green’s kernel at some
w(t)
is not well defined, so we instead consider the pullback under
w
. The ill-defined expression
\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}G_{x}(w(t))f(\frac{t}{s})dt
can then be replaced with
w^{*}(G_{x})[f(\tfrac{\cdot}{s})]
(in the case
E=M\times{\mathbb{C}}
; for vector bundles, we need to take a bundle coordinate).

We start by fixing some notation:

Definition 4.2.1
.

For a function
f
on
{\mathbb{R}}
, let

(f)_{odd}(t):=\frac{1}{2}(f(t)-f(-t))

and

(f)_{even}(t):=\frac{1}{2}(f(t)+f(-t))

denote the odd and even part of
f
. If
f
is defined only on a subset of
{\mathbb{R}}
, extend it by
0
.

Fixed Notation 4.2.2
.

For the remainder of this section, let
U\subseteq M
be open and convex,
I\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}
an open interval containing
0
and
x\in U
. Furthermore, let
w\colon I\rightarrow U
be a future oriented smooth curve with
w(0)=x
.

In several of the following theorems, there will be a ”more precisely” part that keeps track of estimates for the remainder terms. These will be needed later on to provide some sort of uniformity for global considerations, but for understanding what is going on, the reader may focus on the less precise first statements.

Definition 4.2.3
.

Define
\nu_{w}\colon I\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}
via

\nu_{w}(t):=\frac{\Gamma^{U}(w(t))}{t^{2}},

extended continuously to
0
.

In some sense
\nu_{w}
measures the failure of
w
to be a geodesic. Later on, this
\nu_{w}
will be a problem and we will have to restrict to the case where
w
is a geodesic. For now, however, we include this for the sake of generality.

Lemma 4.2.4
.

\nu_{w}
is a well-defined, smooth, strictly positive function and

\nu_{w}(0)=\gamma(w^{\prime}(0)).

If
w
is a timelike unit speed geodesic, we have

\nu_{w}=1

constantly.

Remark 4.2.5
.

Unfortunately, the presence of
\nu_{w}
will pose problems later on, so we will eventually have to restrict to the case where
w
is a unit speed geodesic.

Proof.

Let
\tilde{w}:=\exp_{x}^{-1}\circ w
. We have

\Gamma^{U}(w(t))=\gamma(\tilde{w}(t))=\gamma(\tilde{w}(0))+(\gamma\circ\tilde{w})^{\prime}(0)t+E(t)t^{2},

where
E
is a smooth function with

E(0)=\frac{1}{2}(\gamma\circ\tilde{w})^{\prime\prime}(0).

As
\tilde{w}(0)=0
, we have

\gamma(\tilde{w}(0))=-g(\tilde{w},\tilde{w})(0)=0,

(\gamma\circ\tilde{w})^{\prime}(0)=-2g(\tilde{w},\tilde{w}^{\prime})(0)=0

and

(\gamma\circ\tilde{w})^{\prime\prime}(0)=-2(g(\tilde{w}^{\prime},\tilde{w}^{\prime})+g(\tilde{w},\tilde{w}^{\prime\prime}))(0)=-2g(w^{\prime}(0),w^{\prime}(0))=2\gamma(w^{\prime}(0)),

where we used that
\tilde{w}^{\prime}(0)
coincides with
w^{\prime}(0)
(when canonically identifying
T_{0}T_{x}M
and
T_{x}M
), as
d\exp_{x}
at
x
is the identity. We thus conclude that

\frac{\Gamma^{U}(w(t))}{t^{2}}=E(t)

extends smoothly to
0
, where it takes the value

E(0)=\gamma(w^{\prime}(0))

(which is strictly positive, as
w^{\prime}(0)
must be timelike). For
t\neq 0
,
w(t)
is in the interior of
J_{+}(x)
or
J_{-}(x)
and thus
\tilde{w}(t)
is in the interior of
J_{+}(0)
or
J_{-}(0)
(i.e. timelike), where
\gamma
is strictly positive. Thus
\gamma(\tilde{w}(t))
and hence
\nu_{w}(t)
are strictly positive.

If
w
is a timelike geodesic, we have
\tilde{w}(t)=tw^{\prime}(0)
, as the exponential map maps geodesics through zero to geodesics through
x
with the same initial derivative. Thus we have

\nu_{w}(t)=\frac{\gamma(tw^{\prime}(0))}{t^{2}}=\gamma(w^{\prime}(0))

if
w
is additionally unit speed, this is
1
.
∎

We shall use a variant of the Mellin transform where some poles are removed:

Definition 4.2.6
.

In the following we abbreviate:

\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(g):=\frac{\mathcal{M}(g)}{\Gamma(\frac{\cdot+1}{2})},

where
\mathcal{M}
denotes the Mellin transform (see subsection
2.1.5
).

For odd smooth
g
, the Gamma function in the denominator will cancel the poles of the Mellin transform, allowing us to evaluate the modified Mellin transform everywhere.
We want to insert the Hadamard expansion into
w^{*}G_{x}[f(\tfrac{\cdot}{s})]
. We start by evaluating the pull-back of a single summand
V^{k}R(2k+2)
. The
W
in the following should thus be thought of as a Hadamard coefficient (or a component thereof).

Proposition 4.2.7
.

For any
W\in C^{\infty}(U)
and
\alpha\in{\mathbb{C}}
,
w^{*}(WR^{U}(\alpha,x))
is well-defined and we have for any
g\in C_{c}^{\infty}(I)
:

w^{*}(WR^{U}(\alpha,x))[g]=\frac{2^{2-\alpha}\pi^{\frac{2-d}{2}}}{\Gamma(\frac{\alpha}{2})}\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\left(\left(\nu_{w}^{\frac{\alpha-d}{2}}(W\circ w)g\right)_{odd}\right)({\alpha-d+1}).

Proof.

Let again
\tilde{w}:=exp_{x}^{-1}\circ w
. As
w^{*}=\tilde{w}^{*}\circ exp_{x}^{*}
, it suffices to show that the pull-back of
exp_{x}^{*}R^{U}(\alpha,x)=R(\alpha)
under
\tilde{w}
is well defined with the correct value. By Theorem
4.1.11
,
R(\alpha)
is holomorphic in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
. Recall that
\Lambda
only contains causal vectors at lightlike or zero basepoints.

For
t\neq 0
,
w(t)
can be reached from
x
by a timelike geodesic by Proposition
2.2.20
. Thus
\tilde{w}(t)
is not lightlike, so
\Lambda|_{\tilde{w}(t)}
is empty. This means if
v\in\Lambda|_{\tilde{w}(t)}
, then
t=0
and
v
is causal. For any non-zero vector
\lambda\partial_{t}
in
T_{0}({\mathbb{R}})
, we have

\left<(d\tilde{w}_{0})^{*}v,\lambda\partial_{t}\right>=\lambda\left<v,\tilde{w}^{\prime}(0)\right>.

The scalar product is non-zero, as
v
is causal and
\tilde{w}^{\prime}(0)
is timelike and thus, by
2.2.3
, they cannot be orthogonal. Thus
\tilde{w}^{*}\Lambda
does not intersect the zero section, whence
\tilde{w}^{*}
maps
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda}(T_{x}(M))
continuously into
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(I)
.

Assume
\Re(\alpha)>d
. Then
R^{U}(\alpha,x)
is continuous. We have

\Gamma^{U}(w(t))=\nu_{w}(t)t^{2}.

Thus we can compute

\displaystyle w^{*}(WR^{U}(\alpha,x))[g]

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{I}W(w(t))c_{\alpha}\sign(t)\Gamma^{U}(w(t))^{\frac{\alpha-d}{2}}g(t)dt

\displaystyle=c_{\alpha}\int\limits_{I}\sign(t)W(w(t))\nu_{w}(t)^{\frac{\alpha-d}{2}}|t|^{\alpha-d}g(t)dt

\displaystyle=2c_{\alpha}\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}\left(\nu_{w}^{\frac{\alpha-d}{2}}(W\circ w)g\right)_{odd}(t)t^{\alpha-d}dt

\displaystyle=2c_{\alpha}\mathcal{M}\left(\left(\nu_{w}^{\frac{\alpha-d}{2}}(W\circ w)g\right)_{odd}\right)({\alpha-d+1})

\displaystyle=\frac{2^{2-\alpha}\pi^{\frac{2-d}{2}}}{\Gamma(\frac{\alpha}{2})}\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\left(\left(\nu_{w}^{\frac{\alpha-d}{2}}(W\circ w)g\right)_{odd}\right)({\alpha-d+1}).

We show that this is true for arbitrary
\alpha
by showing that both sides are holomorphic.

Multiplication with a smooth function is a continuous map of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda}(T_{x}M)
to itself. Evaluation of a distribution is also continuous. As continuous linear maps preserve holomorphicity and the family of Riesz distributions is holomorphic in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda}(T_{x}M)
, we can conclude that
\tilde{w}^{*}((W\circ exp_{x})R(\alpha))[g]=w^{*}(WR^{U}(\alpha,x))[g]
is holomorphic.

For the right hand side, consider the family
X_{\alpha}
as in Proposition
4.1.10
. This is holomorphic on
\Re(\alpha)>0
both in
D^{\prime}({\mathbb{R}})
and
C^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}\backslash\{0\})
, as
C^{+}_{\infty}
embeds continuously into both spaces.
As

X_{\alpha-1}=\frac{1}{\alpha}X_{\alpha}^{\prime},

it extends meromorphically to all of
{\mathbb{C}}
. By meromorphic continuation,

\mathcal{M}(f)(\alpha)=X_{\alpha-1}[f]

holds on all of
{\mathbb{C}}
. We thus have

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}\left(\left(\nu_{w}^{\frac{\alpha-d}{2}}(W\circ w)g\right)_{odd}\right)({\alpha-d+1})

\displaystyle=X_{\alpha-d}\left[\left((X_{\frac{\alpha-d}{2}}\circ\nu_{w})(W\circ w)g\right)_{odd}\right].

As
\nu_{w}
is strictly positive, the argument is meromorphic in
C^{\infty}_{c}({\mathbb{R}})
. As evaluation is hypocontinuous, the result is meromorphic.

This implies that both sides of the claimed result are meromorphic in
\alpha
, so equality holds for all
\alpha\in{\mathbb{C}}
(with the fraction of meromorphic functions being extended continuously to
\alpha
where both numerator and denominator have a pole).
∎

In order to retain more information after distributional evaluation, we equip the function we evaluate at with an additional parameter
s
.

Fixed Notation 4.2.8
.

Let
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})
be an odd function. For
s>0
and
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, define

f_{s}(t):=f\left(\frac{t}{s}\right).

Let
I_{f}
be a closed finite interval whose interior contains
0
and
\supp(f)
.

This
f
corresponds roughly to (the derivative of the Fourier transform of) the cutoff function in the spectral action,
s
corresponding to the cutoff parameter there. The
f
here being odd corresponds to that in the spectral action being even.
The assumption that
f
is odd is not crucial for the following considerations, but it makes things somewhat simpler and adding an even part would not yield anything helpful for our purposes (see remark
5.2.19
).

Testing a distribution against
f_{s}
for small
s
corresponds to investigating its behaviour around
0
. Multiplying with
f_{s}
and taking the Mellin transform does something similar. This is investigated in the following two lemmas.

Lemma 4.2.9
.

If
h
is a
C^{k+1}
function whose first
k
derivatives at
0
vanish and
\alpha\in{\mathbb{C}}
with
\Re(\alpha)\geq-k
, we have for
s\in(0,1)

|\mathcal{M}(hf_{s})(\alpha)|\leq C\|h\|_{C^{k}(I_{f})}s^{k+\Re(\alpha)}

and

\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}h(t)f_{s}(t)dt\leq C\|h\|_{C^{k}(I_{f})}s^{k+1},

where
C
is a constant depending on
\alpha
,
k
and
f
.

Proof.

By Taylor’s theorem (with vanishing Taylor series), we have

h(t)=t^{k}E(t)

for some continuous function
E
.
|E|
is bounded on
I_{f}
by
C\|h\|_{C^{k}(I_{f})}
. We then have for
s\in(0,1)
:

\displaystyle|M(hf_{s})(\alpha)|

\displaystyle=|M(Ef_{s})(\alpha+k)|

\displaystyle\leq\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}\|h\|_{C^{k}(I_{f})}|t^{k+\alpha-1}f\left(\frac{t}{s}\right)|dt

\displaystyle=|s^{k+\alpha}|\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{s}\|h\|_{C^{k}(I_{f})}|\left(\frac{t}{s}\right)^{k+\alpha-1}f\left(\frac{t}{s}\right)|dt

\displaystyle=\|h\|_{C^{k}(I_{f})}|s^{k+\alpha}|\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}|t^{k+\alpha-1}f(t)|dt

\displaystyle=:C\|h\|_{C^{k}(I_{f})}s^{k+\Re(\alpha)}.

The second part of the claim follows either by doing an analogous calculation or by observing that

\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}h(t)f_{s}(t)dt=\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}(h(t)-h(-t))f_{s}(t)dt=M((h-h(-\cdot))f_{s})(1).\qed

Lemma 4.2.10
.

Let
h
be a smooth function defined on
I
and
\alpha\in{\mathbb{C}}
. Then

\mathcal{M}^{\prime}((hf_{s})_{odd})(\alpha)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s\rightarrow 0}}{{\sim}}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{k!}{(2k)!}\binom{\frac{\alpha+2k-1}{2}}{k}h^{(2k)}(0)\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(f)(\alpha+2k)s^{\alpha+2k}.

More precisely, we have for
s
small enough such that
f_{s}\subseteq C_{c}^{\infty}(I^{\prime})
for
I^{\prime}\subseteq I
closed and
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
:

\left|\mathcal{M}^{\prime}((hf_{s})_{odd})(\alpha)-\sum\limits_{k=0}^{N}\frac{k!}{(2k)!}\binom{\frac{\alpha+2k-1}{2}}{k}h^{(2k)}(0)\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(f)(\alpha+2k)s^{\alpha+2k}\right|\leq C\|h\|_{C^{2N}(I^{\prime})}s^{2N+\Re(\alpha)},

for some constant
C
depending on
N
,
\alpha
,
f
and
I^{\prime}
.

Proof.

As all even derivatives of
(hf_{s})_{odd}
at
0
vanish, its Mellin transform has poles only at odd negative integers.
\Gamma(\frac{\cdot+1}{2})
also has poles there, so the quotient is defined everywhere.

Write
t^{k}
for the function
t\mapsto t^{k}
. For
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
\Re(\alpha)\geq 0
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(t^{k}f_{s})(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\frac{\alpha+1}{2})}\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}t^{\alpha+k-1}f\left(\frac{t}{s}\right)dt

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\frac{\alpha+1}{2})}s^{\alpha+k}\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{s}\left(\frac{t}{s}\right)^{\alpha+k-1}f\left(\frac{t}{s}\right)dt

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\frac{\alpha+1}{2})}s^{\alpha+k}\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}t^{\alpha+k-1}f(t)dt

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\frac{\alpha+1}{2})}\mathcal{M}(f)(\alpha+k)s^{\alpha+k}.

By analytic continuation, this holds for all
\alpha\in{\mathbb{C}}
. Now fix
\alpha\in{\mathbb{C}}
. As
f
is odd, we have
(hf_{s})_{odd}=h_{even}f_{s}
.
We write
(h)_{even}
in a Taylor expansion

(h)_{even}(t)=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{N}\frac{1}{(2k)!}h^{(2k)}(0)t^{2k}+E(t),

where the first
2N
