derivatives of
E
at
0
vanish and

\|E\|_{C^{k}(I)}\leq C\|h\|_{C^{k}(I)}

for any
k\geq 2N
.
We obtain for
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
2N\geq-\Re(\alpha)
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{\prime}((hf_{s}))(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{N}\frac{1}{(2k)!}h^{(2k)}(0)\left(\frac{\Gamma(\frac{\cdot+2k+1}{2})\mathcal{M}(t^{2k}f_{s})}{\Gamma(\frac{\cdot+2k+1}{2})\Gamma(\frac{\cdot+1}{2})}\right)(\alpha)+\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(E(t)f_{s})(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{N}\frac{k!}{(2k)!}h^{(2k)}(0)\left(\frac{\Gamma(\frac{\cdot+1}{2}+k)}{k!\Gamma(\frac{\cdot+1}{2})}\frac{\mathcal{M}(f_{s})(\cdot+2k)}{\Gamma(\frac{\cdot+2k+1}{2})}\right)(\alpha)+\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(E(t)f_{s})(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{N}\frac{k!}{(2k)!}\binom{\frac{\alpha+2k-1}{2}}{k}h^{(2k)}(0)\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(f)(\alpha+2k)s^{\alpha+2k}+\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(E(t)f_{s})(\alpha)

Using the Lemma
4.2.9
for the remainder term, we get

\frac{1}{\Gamma(\frac{\alpha+1}{2})}\mathcal{M}(E(t)f_{s})(\alpha)\leq C\|h\|_{C^{2N}(I)}s^{2N+\Re(\alpha)}

for some
C\in{\mathbb{R}}
, as desired.
∎

The coefficients appearing in the next theorem will occur several times throughout this thesis, so we shall give them a name for later reference.

Definition 4.2.11
.

For
k,n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, define

a(k,n):=a(k,n,f,d):=\frac{\pi^{\frac{2-d}{2}}n!}{4^{k}k!(2n)!}\binom{k+n+1-\frac{d}{2}}{n}\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(f)({2k+2n+3-d}).

Putting together what we have done so far, we obtain an abstract preliminary version of this sections main result:

Theorem 4.2.12
.

Assume that
K
is a distribution on
U
that vanishes outside of
J(x)
and there are
W_{k}\in C^{\infty}(U)
such that

K\sim\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}W_{k}R^{U}(2k+2,x).

Then
w^{*}(K)
is well-defined and we have

\displaystyle w^{*}(K)[f_{s}]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s\rightarrow 0}}{{\sim}}

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}a(k,n)\left(\nu_{w}^{k-\tfrac{d}{2}+1}W_{k}\circ w\right)^{(2n)}(0)s^{2k+2n+3-d}

for
a(k,n)
as in Definition
4.2.11
.

More precisely, if
s
is small enough such that
f_{s}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(I^{\prime})
for
I^{\prime}\subseteq I
closed and if we fix
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we have for
N^{\prime},N\in{\mathbb{N}}
large enough the following estimate for the remainder terms:

\displaystyle\left|w^{*}(K)[f_{s}]-\sum\limits_{k=0}^{N}\sum\limits_{n=0}^{N^{\prime}}a(k,n)\left(\nu_{w}^{k-\tfrac{d}{2}+1}W_{k}\circ w\right)^{(2n)}(0)s^{2k+2n+3-d}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\left(N^{\prime}\max\limits_{k\leq N^{\prime}}\Big\|\nu_{w}^{k-\tfrac{d}{2}+1}W_{k}\circ w\Big\|_{C^{N^{\prime}}(I^{\prime})}+\Big\|\Big(K-\sum\limits_{k=0}^{N}W_{k}R^{U}(2k+2,x)\Big)\circ w\Big\|_{C^{m}(I^{\prime})}\right)s^{m}

Remark 4.2.13
.

We mainly want to use this with
G_{x}
and
V_{x}^{k,U}
instead of
K
and
W_{k}
. However, we keep this general so that it could also be applied to other expansions, like, for example, that for
G^{\pm m}_{x}
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we may assume that
I_{f}\subseteq I
, otherwise rescale
f
until it is supported in
I
. Let
s\in(0,1)
. Let
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
be arbitrary and let
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
be large enough such that

F:=K-\sum\limits_{k=0}^{N}(W_{k}R^{U}(2k+2,x))\in C^{m}(U).

As both the sum and the remainder have well-defined pull-backs under
w
, the same is true for
K
. As both
K
and all
R^{U}(2k+2,x)
vanish outside of
J(x)
, the same is true for
F
and thus also for its derivatives. As
x\in\partial J(x)
by Proposition
2.2.14
, this means that all derivatives of order up to
m
of
F
vanish at
x
. Therefore the first
m
derivatives of
w^{*}F=F\circ w
at 0 vanish.
By Lemma
4.2.9
, we can conclude that

|w^{*}(F)[f_{s}]|=\Big|\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}F\circ w(t)f_{s}(t)dt\Big|\leq C\|F\circ w\|_{C^{m}(I_{f})}s^{m}.

For each of the summands, we can apply Proposition
4.2.7
and Lemma
4.2.10
to obtain for
N^{\prime}\geq\frac{m+d}{2}
and
k<N
:

\displaystyle w^{*}(W_{k}R^{U}(2k+2,x))[f_{s}]

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi^{\frac{2-d}{2}}}{4^{k}k!}\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\left(\left((\nu_{w}^{k-\tfrac{d}{2}+1}W_{k}\circ w)f_{s}\right)_{odd}\right)({2k+3-d})

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi^{\frac{2-d}{2}}}{4^{k}k!}\sum\limits_{n=0}^{N^{\prime}}\frac{n!}{(2n)!}\binom{k+n+1-\frac{d}{2}}{n}\left(\nu_{w}^{k-\tfrac{d}{2}+1}W_{k}\circ w\right)^{(2n)}(0)\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(f)({2k+2n+3-d})s^{2k+2n+3-d}+F_{k}

with

|F_{k}|\leq C\|\nu_{w}^{k-\tfrac{d}{2}+1}W_{k}\circ w\|_{C^{2N^{\prime}}(I_{f})}s^{2N^{\prime}+2k+3-d}\leq C\|\nu_{w}^{k-\tfrac{d}{2}+1}W_{k}\circ w\|_{C^{2N^{\prime}}(I_{f})}s^{m}.

We then have

\displaystyle w^{*}(K)[f_{s}]

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{N}w^{*}(W_{k}R^{U}(2k+2,x))[f_{s}]+w^{*}(F)[f_{s}]

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{N}\frac{\pi^{\frac{2-d}{2}}}{4^{k}k!}\sum\limits_{n=0}^{N^{\prime}}\frac{n!}{(2n)!}\binom{k+n+1-\frac{d}{2}}{n}\left(\nu_{w}^{k-\tfrac{d}{2}+1}W_{k}\circ w\right)^{(2n)}(0)

\displaystyle\cdot\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(f)({2k+2n+3-d})s^{2k+2n+3-d}+F_{k}+w^{*}(F)[f_{s}]

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{N}\sum\limits_{n=0}^{N^{\prime}}\frac{\pi^{\frac{2-d}{2}}n!}{4^{k}k!(2n)!}\binom{k+n+1-\frac{d}{2}}{n}\left(\nu_{w}^{k-\tfrac{d}{2}+1}W_{k}\circ w\right)^{(2n)}(0)

\displaystyle\cdot\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(f)({2k+2n+3-d})s^{2k+2n+3-d}+O(s^{m}).

As
m
was arbitrary, this concludes the proof, the more precise estimate following from the estimates for the error terms made above.
∎

We want to apply this theorem with
G_{x}
and the Hadamard coefficients. This works immediately if
P
acts on scalar functions. In general, we get problems with “integrating” a function that takes values in different fibres of a vector bundle. To solve this, we compose with an arbitrary bundle coordinate. We then get this chapter’s main result:

Theorem 4.2.14
.

Assume that
U
is a GE set. Let
A:E\otimes E_{x}^{*}\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}
be an arbitrary bundle coordinate. Then
w^{*}(AG_{x})
is well-defined and we have

\displaystyle w^{*}(A(G_{x}))[f_{s}]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s\rightarrow 0}}{{\sim}}

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}a(k,n)\left(\nu_{w}^{k-\tfrac{d}{2}+1}AV^{k,U}_{x}\circ w\right)^{(2n)}(0)s^{2k+2n+3-d}

with
a(k,n)
as in Definition
4.2.11
.

More precisely, for
s
small enough such that
f_{s}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(I^{\prime})
with
I^{\prime}\subseteq I
closed and for every
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
, if we choose
N,N^{\prime}
large enough, we can estimate the remainder terms by:

\displaystyle\left|w^{*}(A(G_{x}))[f_{s}]-\sum\limits_{k=0}^{N}\sum\limits_{n=0}^{N^{\prime}}a(k,n)\left(\nu_{w}^{k-\tfrac{d}{2}+1}AV^{k,U}_{x}\circ w\right)^{(2n)}(0)s^{2k+2n+3-d}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\max\limits_{k\leq N^{\prime}}\Big\|\nu_{w}^{k-\tfrac{d}{2}+1}AV^{k,U}_{x}\circ w\Big\|_{C^{N^{\prime}}(I)}+\Big\|A\Big(G_{x}-\sum\limits_{k=0}^{N}V^{k,U}_{x}R^{U}(2k+2,x)\Big)\circ w\Big\|_{C^{m}(I^{\prime})}\right)s^{m}.

Proof.

As
A
is linear, preserves differentiability orders and we can pull out scalar distributions, we have on
U

AG_{x}\sim\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}(A(V^{k,U}_{x}))R^{U}(2k+2,x).

Thus we can apply
4.2.12
to obtain the desired result.
∎

Now we will need the remainder estimates that we have been dragging around with us. These give us the uniformity we need to integrate this asymptotic expanson, giving the following generalization of the result:

Theorem 4.2.15
.

Let
M^{\prime}\subset M
be any submanifold and let
\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(M^{\prime})
be arbitrary. Let
A
be any bundle coordinate on
E\boxtimes E^{*}
and let
A_{x}
be its restriction to
E\otimes E^{*}_{x}
.
Let
(w_{x})_{x\in M^{\prime}}
be a family of timelike curves defined on an interval
I
containing
0
such that
w_{x}(0)=x
and

\phi(x,t):=w_{x}(t)

is smooth. Then we have

\displaystyle\int\limits_{M^{\prime}}\chi(x)w_{x}^{*}(A_{x}G_{x})[f_{s}]dx\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s\rightarrow 0}}{{\sim}}

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}a(k,n)\int\limits_{M^{\prime}}\chi(x)\left(\nu_{w_{x}}^{k-\tfrac{d}{2}+1}A_{x}V^{k}_{x}\circ w_{x}\right)^{(2n)}(0)dx\ s^{2k+2n+3-d}

with
a(k,n)
as in Definition
4.2.11
.

Proof.

Any
x\in\supp(\chi)
has a GE neighborhood
U_{x}
in
M
by Proposition
3.1.8
. As
\phi^{-1}(U)
is open, we can find a relatively compact neighborhood
O_{x}
of
x
in
M^{\prime}
and
s_{x}>0
such that
\phi(O_{x}\times s_{x}I_{f})\subseteq U_{x}
. As
\supp(\chi)
is compact, there is a finite subcover
(O_{x})_{x\in J}
of
\supp(\chi)
. Choose

s_{0}\leq\min\limits_{x\in J}s_{x},

such that
s_{0}I_{f}\subseteq I
. In the following, assume
s<s_{0}
.
By choice of
s_{0}
, we see that for any
t\in s_{0}I_{f}
and
y\in\supp(\chi)
,
w_{y}(t)
is well-defined and inside a GE neighborhood of
y
independent of
t
. Restricting
w_{y}
to a neighborhood of
s_{0}I_{f}
that still gets mapped to this
GE
neighborhood, we can apply the local result at each point.

Let
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
be arbitrary and choose sufficiently large
N
and
N^{\prime}
as in Theorem
4.2.14
. For any
i\in J
, we can then estimate

\displaystyle\int\limits_{O_{i}}|\chi(x)|\Big|w_{x}^{*}(A_{x}G_{x})[f_{s}]-\sum\limits_{k=0}^{N}\sum\limits_{n=0}^{N^{\prime}}a(k,n)\left(\nu_{w_{x}}^{k-\tfrac{d}{2}+1}A_{x}V^{k,U_{i}}_{x}\circ w_{x}\right)^{(2n)}(0)\ s^{2k+2n+3-d}\Big|dx

\displaystyle\leq\int\limits_{O_{i}}|\chi(x)|C\Big(N^{\prime}\max\limits_{k\leq N^{\prime}}\|\nu_{w_{x}}^{k-\tfrac{d}{2}+1}A_{x}V^{k,U_{i}}_{x}\circ w_{x}\|_{C^{N^{\prime}}(s_{0}I_{f})}

\displaystyle+\|A_{x}(G_{x}-\sum\limits_{k=0}^{N}V^{k,U_{i}}_{x}R^{U_{i}}(2k+2,x))\circ w_{x}\|_{C^{m}(s_{0}I_{f})}\Big)dx\ s^{m}

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime}s^{m}.

In the last estimate, we used that
O_{i}
is relatively compact and thus for any
C^{K}
-function
g\colon I\times O_{i}\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}
, we have

\sup_{x\in O_{i}}\|g(\cdot,x)\|_{C^{K}(s_{0}I_{f})}\leq\|g\|_{C^{K}(s_{0}I_{f}\times O_{i})}<\infty.

As the
O_{i}
cover
\supp(\chi)
, we can conclude

\displaystyle\Big|\int\limits_{M^{\prime}}\chi(x)w_{x}^{*}(A_{x}G_{x})[f_{s}]dx-\sum\limits_{k=0}^{N}\sum\limits_{n=0}^{N^{\prime}}a(k,n)\int\limits_{M^{\prime}}\chi(x)\left(\nu_{w_{x}}^{k-\tfrac{d}{2}+1}A_{x}V^{k,U_{i}}_{x}\circ w_{x}\right)^{(2n)}(0)dx\ s^{2k+2n+3-d}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{i\in I}\int\limits_{O_{i}}|\chi(x)|\Big|\chi(x)w_{x}^{*}(A_{x}G_{x})[f_{s}]-\sum\limits_{k=0}^{N}\sum\limits_{n=0}^{N^{\prime}}a(k,n)\left(\nu_{w_{x}}^{k-\tfrac{d}{2}+1}A_{x}V^{k,U_{i}}_{x}\circ w_{x}\right)^{(2n)}(0)\ s^{2k+2n+3-d}\Big|dx

\displaystyle\leq Cs^{m}.

As
m
was arbitrary, this concludes the proof.
∎

Chapter 5
Extracting Hadamard coefficients

We now seek to use the result of the previous chapter to obtain the Hadamard coefficients on the diagonal, i.e.
V^{k}_{x}(x)
. The problem with the asymptotic expansion we derived is that we cannot distinguish between the contribution coming from the
k
-th Hadamard coefficient and that from the
2j
-th derivative of the
(k-j)
-th coefficient. This problem does not arise from the choice of action-like function we construct, but is inherent in the asymptotic expansion we are using.

The problem is similar to that of determining the coefficients of a power series

\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}a_{k}(x)x^{k}

where the coefficients are themselves depending on
x
. Not even the values of the coefficients at
x=0
(except for the first) are determined by this expansion, as we may always add some function
g
to one of the coefficients and subtract
xg
from the previous one without changing the sum.

This problem also occurs with the Hadamard expansion: As

\Gamma R(2k,x)=2k(2k-d+2)R(2k+2,x),

we may (for
k\notin\{0,\frac{d}{2}-1\}
) add some
g
to
V^{k}_{x}
and subtract
\frac{\Gamma g}{2k(2k-d+2)}
from
V^{k+1}_{x}
without changing the sum in the asymptotic expansion of
G_{x}
. Thus
V^{0}
and
V^{\frac{d}{2}-1}
(in case
d
is even) are the only Hadamard coefficients whose value at the diagonal we may hope to obtain by using only this expansion (and those we actually do obtain from our procedure).

We thus need to include some extra information about the Hadamard coefficients, if we want to succeed. We do this by looking at the Hadamard expansion of
P-z
for variable
z\in{\mathbb{C}}
and using Proposition
3.2.4
to express the Hadamard coefficients of
P-z
in terms of those of
P
. An alternative approach would be to use the asymptotic expansion for powers of
G^{\pm}
instead.

5.1
Hadamard coefficients for
k<\frac{d}{2}
and
d
even

In case
d
is even, the coefficient
V^{\frac{d}{2}-1}_{x}(x)
can be read off fairly immediately, either directly from the Green’s kernel or from the expansion of the action analogue that we constructed. Knowing this coefficient for any
P-z
also gives us all coefficients of lower order. We shall elaborate on this first, before we continue with deriving our more general but also more complicated formula for arbitrary coefficients. A further advantage of the formula in this special case is that it works for arbitrary timelike paths, while the more general formula only works for geodesics.

We will need some notation for the coefficients to specific monomials in a polynomial or an asymptotic expansion.

Definition 5.1.1
.

For a function
F
with an asymptotic expansion

F(s)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s\rightarrow 0}}{{\sim}}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}a_{k}s^{b_{k}}

with
(b_{k})
strictly increasing, we define

F[[b_{k}]]:=F(s)[[s^{b_{k}}]]:=a_{k}.

If instead
F
depends on two parameters, we define

F[[\alpha,\beta]]:=F(s,z)[[s^{\alpha}z^{\beta}]]:=(F(s,z)[[s^{\alpha}]])[[z^{\beta}]],

whenever the right hand side is defined.

Note that polynomials are a special case of an asymptotic expansion (with almost all summands equal to zero). As the asyptotic expansion used in this definition is unique (up to adding summands with zero coefficients),
F[[b_{k}]]
is well-defined. It can be calculated by looking at the residues of the Mellin transform of
F
. In case all
b_{k}
are of the form
b_{k}=b_{0}+n_{k}
for
n_{k}\in{\mathbb{N}}
(as will always be the case here), they can be calculated more easily as

F[[b_{0}+n]]=\frac{1}{n!}\partial_{s}^{n}s^{-b_{0}}F(s)|_{s=0}.

We now proceed to show how the first few hadamard coefficients in even dimensions can be computed from the Green’s kernel.

Proposition 5.1.2
.

Assume that
d
is even and
w
is a future oriented timelike curve contained in a GE subset
U
of
M
with
w(0)=x
. Then for
t\neq 0
,
G_{x}
is a continuous function around
w(t)
and we have

V^{\frac{d}{2}-1}_{x}(x)=\lim\limits_{t\rightarrow 0}\ 2\sign(t)(4\pi)^{\frac{d}{2}-1}(\tfrac{d}{2}-1)!G_{x}(w(t)).

More generally, we have for
k<\frac{d}{2}

V_{x}^{k}(x)=2(\tfrac{d}{2}-1-k)!k!(4\pi)^{\frac{d}{2}-1}\Big(\lim\limits_{t\rightarrow 0}\ \sign(t)G_{P-z,x}(w(t))\Big)[[z^{\frac{d}{2}-1-k}]].

Proof.

We have on
U

G_{x}=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\frac{d}{2}-1}V_{x}^{k}R^{U}(2k+2,x)+E

for some continuous function
E
(it suffices to sum up to
\frac{d}{2}-1
, as all higher order Riesz distributions are continuous). For
n<d
even, the Riesz distributions
R^{U}(n,x)
(for even
d
) are supported on the boundary of
J^{\pm}
(see
[
BGP07
]
, Proposition 1.4.2 (8)).
w(t)
for
t\neq 0
is in the timelike future or past of
x
, i.e. the interior of
J^{\pm}
(recall that by
2.2.20
, the causal and timelike futures of
x
are the images of the corresponding cones in
T_{x}M
, so everything works out). Thus all summands for
k<\frac{d}{2}-1
in the expansion vanish around
w(t)
.
As

R^{U}(d,x)=c_{d}\Gamma^{0}(\mathbbm{1}_{J_{+}(x)}-\mathbbm{1}_{J_{-}(x)})=\frac{(4\pi)^{1-\frac{d}{2}}}{2(\frac{d}{2}-1)!}(\mathbbm{1}_{J_{+}(x)}-\mathbbm{1}_{J_{-}(x)})

is constant in the future/past of
x
(and thus around
w(t)
), we can conclude that, in a neighborhood of
w(t)
,
G_{x}
coincides with the continuous function

\frac{(4\pi)^{1-\frac{d}{2}}}{2(\tfrac{d}{2}-1)!}\sign(t)V_{x}^{\frac{d}{2}-1}+E.

As
E
has to be zero outside of
J(x)
(because the same is true for
G_{x}
and the Riesz distributions), it vanishes at
x
. Since
E
is continuous, we can conclude that

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{t\rightarrow 0}\ \sign(t)G_{x}(w(t))

\displaystyle=\lim\limits_{t\rightarrow 0}\frac{(4\pi)^{1-\frac{d}{2}}}{2(\tfrac{d}{2}-1)!}V_{x}^{\frac{d}{2}-1}(w(t))+\sign(t)E(w(t))

\displaystyle=\frac{(4\pi)^{1-\frac{d}{2}}}{2(\tfrac{d}{2}-1)!}V_{x}^{\frac{d}{2}-1}(x).

This implies the first claim.

Applying this for
P-z
with
z\in{\mathbb{C}}
and using
3.2.4
, we get for
k<\frac{d}{2}

\displaystyle\Big(\lim\limits_{t\rightarrow 0}\ \sign(t)G_{P-z,x}(w(t))\Big)[[z^{\frac{d}{2}-1-k}]]

\displaystyle=\frac{(4\pi)^{1-\frac{d}{2}}}{2(\frac{d}{2}-1)!}\Big(V_{x}^{k}(z)(x)\Big)[[z^{\frac{d}{2}-1-k}]]

\displaystyle=\frac{(4\pi)^{1-\frac{d}{2}}}{2(\frac{d}{2}-1)!}\Big(\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\frac{d}{2}-1}\binom{\frac{d}{2}-1}{n}z^{\frac{d}{2}-1-n}V_{x}^{n}(x)\Big)[[z^{\frac{d}{2}-1-k}]]

\displaystyle=\frac{(4\pi)^{1-\frac{d}{2}}}{2(\frac{d}{2}-1)!}\binom{\frac{d}{2}-1}{k}V_{x}^{k}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{(4\pi)^{1-\frac{d}{2}}}{2(\frac{d}{2}-1-k)!k!}V_{x}^{k}(x).

Thus we have

V_{x}^{k}(x)=2(\tfrac{d}{2}-1-k)!k!(4\pi)^{\frac{d}{2}-1}\Big(\lim\limits_{t\rightarrow 0}\ \sign(t)G_{P-z,x}(w(t))\Big)[[z^{\frac{d}{2}-1-k}]].\qed

We also get an analogous formula from the asymptotic expansion derived in the previous chapter.

Theorem 5.1.3
.

Assume that
d
is even,
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(f)(1)\neq 0
and the assumptions of Theorem
4.2.15
hold. Then we have for
k<\tfrac{d}{2}
:

\int\limits_{M^{\prime}}\chi(x)A_{x}V^{k}_{x}(x)dx=\frac{(4\pi)^{\frac{d}{2}-1}{(\frac{d}{2}-1-k)}!k!}{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(f)(1)}\int\limits_{M^{\prime}}\chi(x)w_{x}^{*}(A_{x}G_{P-z,x})[f_{s}]dx[[s^{1}z^{\frac{d}{2}-1-k}]].

Remark 5.1.4
.

Taking
M^{\prime}=\{x\}
(and
\chi=1
) we obtain the pointwise result

A_{x}V^{k}_{x}(x)=\frac{(4\pi)^{\frac{d}{2}-1}{(\frac{d}{2}-1-k)}!k!}{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(f)(1)}\big(w_{x}^{*}(A_{x}G_{P-z,x})[f_{s}]\big)[[s^{1}z^{\frac{d}{2}-1-k}]].

Proof.

For integer
n>0
and
k+n=\tfrac{d}{2}-1
, we have

\binom{k+n+1-\frac{d}{2}}{n}=\binom{0}{n}=0

and thus

a_{k,n}=0.

This means that the only summand in the expansion of Theorem
4.2.15
that gives a non-zero contribution of order
s^{1}
(corresponding to
n+k=\frac{d}{2}-1
) is the summand for
k=\tfrac{d}{2}-1
and
n=0
. We thus obtain from
4.2.15
:

\displaystyle\int\limits_{M^{\prime}}\chi(x)w_{x}^{*}(A_{x}G_{x})[f_{s}]dx[[s^{1}]]

\displaystyle=a(\tfrac{d}{2}-1,0)\int\limits_{M^{\prime}}\chi(x)A_{x}V^{\frac{d}{2}-1}_{x}(w_{x}(0))dx

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(f)(1)}{(4\pi)^{\frac{d}{2}-1}{(\frac{d}{2}-1)}!}\int\limits_{M^{\prime}}\chi(x)A_{x}V^{\frac{d}{2}-1}_{x}(x)dx.

Applying this for
P-z
instead of
P
and using Proposition
3.2.4
to express the Hadamard coefficient for
P-z
in terms of those for
P
, we obtain

\displaystyle\int\limits_{M^{\prime}}\chi(x)w_{x}^{*}(A_{x}G_{P-z,x})[f_{s}]dx[[s^{1}z^{m}]]

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(f)(1)}{(4\pi)^{\frac{d}{2}-1}{(\frac{d}{2}-1)}!}\int\limits_{M^{\prime}}\chi(x)A_{x}V^{\frac{d}{2}-1}_{x}(z)(x)dx[[z^{m}]]

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(f)(1)}{(4\pi)^{\frac{d}{2}-1}{(\frac{d}{2}-1)}!}\int\limits_{M^{\prime}}\chi(x)A_{x}\sum\limits_{m^{\prime}=0}^{\frac{d}{2}-1}\binom{\frac{d}{2}-1}{m^{\prime}}V^{\frac{d}{2}-1-m^{\prime}}_{x}(x)z^{m^{\prime}}dx[[z^{m}]]

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(f)(1)}{(4\pi)^{\frac{d}{2}-1}{(\frac{d}{2}-1)}!}\binom{\frac{d}{2}-1}{m}\int\limits_{M^{\prime}}\chi(x)A_{x}V^{\frac{d}{2}-1-m}_{x}(x)dx.

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(f)(1)}{(4\pi)^{\frac{d}{2}-1}{m!(\frac{d}{2}-1-m)}!}\int\limits_{M^{\prime}}\chi(x)A_{x}V^{\frac{d}{2}-1-m}_{x}(x)dx.

Applying this with
m=\frac{d}{2}-1-k
and moving the prefactors to the other side, we obtain

\int\limits_{M^{\prime}}\chi(x)A_{x}V^{k}_{x}(x)dx=\frac{(4\pi)^{\frac{d}{2}-1}{(\frac{d}{2}-1-k)}!k!}{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(f)(1)}\int\limits_{M^{\prime}}\chi(x)w_{x}^{*}(A_{x}G_{P-z,x})[f_{s}]dx[[s^{1}z^{\frac{d}{2}-1-k}]].\qed

In the special case
k=1
, we can use this to obtain a formula for the scalar curvature.

Corollary 5.1.5
.

Let
U
,
w
and
x
be as above. Assume that
P=\square
is the d’Alembertian for the metric
g
(acting on scalar functions). For
d>2
even, the local scalar curvature of
M
is given by

\scal(x)=\frac{6(4\pi)^{\frac{d}{2}-1}(\frac{d}{2}-2)!}{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(f)({1})}\big(w^{*}(G_{\square-z,x})[f_{s}]\big)[[s^{1}z^{\tfrac{d}{2}-2}]].

In the special case
d=4
, we obtain

\scal(x)=\frac{24\pi}{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(f)({1})}\big(w^{*}(G_{\square,x})[f_{s}]\big)[[s^{1}]].

Proof.

We have (see
[
BGP07
]
, last line before Remark 2.3.2)

V^{1}_{x}(x)=\frac{1}{6}\scal(x).

Thus we obtain from the above (with
M^{\prime}=\{x\}
,
A=id
and
K=1
)

\displaystyle\scal(x)

\displaystyle=6V^{1}_{x}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{6(4\pi)^{\frac{d}{2}-1}(\frac{d}{2}-2)!}{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(f)({1})}w^{*}(G_{\square-z,x})[f_{s}][[s^{1}z^{\tfrac{d}{2}-2}]].

In case
d=4
, we can use the fact that
w^{*}(G_{\square-z,x})[f_{s}][[s^{1}]]
has no negative
z
-powers and thus the
z^{0}
-coefficient is just the value at
z=0
. We obtain

\scal(x)=\frac{24\pi}{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(f)({1})}w^{*}(G_{\square,x})[f_{s}][[s^{1}]].\qed

5.2
Arbitrary Hadamard coefficients

We now turn towards deriving a formula for arbitrary Hadamard coefficients. In this case we will have to look at coefficients for various powers of
s
and
z
in the action-like function for
P-z
and combine these via linear algebra.

Proposition 5.2.1
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
4.2.14
, we have for any
z\in{\mathbb{C}}
the following asymptotic expansion in
s
:

\displaystyle w^{*}(A(G_{P-z,x}))[f_{s}]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s\rightarrow 0}}{{\sim}}

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{l,m,n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\pi^{\frac{2-d}{2}}n!}{4^{l+m}l!m!(2n)!}\binom{l+m+n+1-\frac{d}{2}}{n}z^{m}\left(\nu_{w}^{l+m-\tfrac{d}{2}+1}AV^{l}_{x}\circ w\right)^{(2n)}(0)

\displaystyle\cdot\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(f)({2l+2m+2n+3-d})s^{2l+2m+2n+3-d}

Proof.

Using Theorem
4.2.14
and Proposition
3.2.4
, we obtain

\displaystyle w^{*}(A(G_{P-z,x}))[f_{s}]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s\rightarrow 0}}{{\sim}}

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\pi^{\frac{2-d}{2}}n!}{4^{k}k!(2n)!}\binom{k+n+1-\frac{d}{2}}{n}\left(\nu_{w}^{k-\tfrac{d}{2}+1}AV^{k}_{x}(z)\circ w\right)^{(2n)}(0)

\displaystyle\cdot\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(f)({2k+2n+3-d})s^{2k+2n+3-d}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\pi^{\frac{2-d}{2}}n!}{4^{k}k!(2n)!}\binom{k+n+1-\frac{d}{2}}{n}\sum\limits_{m=0}^{k}\binom{k}{m}z^{m}\left(\nu_{w}^{k-\tfrac{d}{2}+1}AV^{k-m}_{x}\circ w\right)^{(2n)}(0)

\displaystyle\cdot\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(f)({2k+2n+3-d})s^{2k+2n+3-d}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{l,m,n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\pi^{\frac{2-d}{2}}n!}{4^{l+m}(l+m)!(2n)!}\binom{l+m+n+1-\frac{d}{2}}{n}\binom{l+m}{m}z^{m}\left(\nu_{w}^{l+m-\tfrac{d}{2}+1}AV^{l}_{x}\circ w\right)^{(2n)}(0)

\displaystyle\cdot\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(f)({2l+2m+2n+3-d})s^{2l+2m+2n+3-d}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{l,m,n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\pi^{\frac{2-d}{2}}n!}{4^{l+m}l!m!(2n)!}\binom{l+m+n+1-\frac{d}{2}}{n}z^{m}\left(\nu_{w}^{l+m-\tfrac{d}{2}+1}AV^{l}_{x}\circ w\right)^{(2n)}(0)

\displaystyle\cdot\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(f)({2l+2m+2n+3-d})s^{2l+2m+2n+3-d}\qed

This is the point where
\nu_{w}
becomes a problem. It makes the expression

\left(\nu_{w}^{l+m-\tfrac{d}{2}+1}AV^{l}_{x}\circ w\right)^{(2n)}(0)

depend on
m
, in a way that can not be easily separated. This would be a problem for our later calculations, so from now on we have to assume that
w
is a timelike unit speed geodesic and hence
\nu_{w}
is
1
, removing the
m
-dependence.
We consider a slightly more general version of the above expansion for such
w
in the following, both to shorten notation and to be able to apply the results not only for the Hadamard coefficients themselves but also for their integral over some subset of
M
.

Fixed Notation 5.2.2
.

For this chapter, assume that
W_{l,n}
are complex numbers for
l,n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, that
I
is an interval containing zero and that

L\colon I\times I\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}

is a function such that we have the asymptotic expansion

\displaystyle L(s,z)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s\rightarrow 0}}{{\sim}}

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{l,m,n=0}^{\infty}\frac{4^{-m}}{m!}\binom{l+m+n+1-\frac{d}{2}}{n}z^{m}W_{l,n}

\displaystyle\cdot\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(f)({2l+2m+2n+3-d})s^{2l+2m+2n+3-d}

The main example is, of course,

L(s,z)=w^{*}(A(G_{P-z,x}))[f_{s}]

and

W_{l,n}=\frac{\pi^{\frac{2-d}{2}}n!}{4^{l}l!(2n)!}\left(AV^{l}_{x}\circ w\right)^{(2n)}(0).

Our goal is to find
W_{k,0}
, which corresponds to a multiple of
AV^{k}_{x}(x)
.
The information we can extract from the asymptotic expansion of
L
consists of the coefficients
L[[k,m]]
. We want to be able to express the
W_{k,0}
in terms of these.

Lemma 5.2.3
.

We have for
K,m\in{\mathbb{N}}
:

L[[2K+2m+3-d,m]]=\sum\limits_{n=0}^{K}\frac{4^{-m}}{m!}\binom{K+m+1-\frac{d}{2}}{n}\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(f)({2K+2m+3-d})W_{K-n,n}

Proof.

As

L(s,z)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s\rightarrow 0}}{{\sim}}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{\ l+n+m^{\prime}=k}\frac{4^{-m^{\prime}}}{m^{\prime}!}\binom{k+1-\frac{d}{2}}{n}z^{m^{\prime}}W_{l,n}\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(f)({2k+3-d})s^{2k+3-d},

we have

\displaystyle L(s,z)[[s^{2K+2m+3-d}]]

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{l+n+m^{\prime}=K+m}\frac{4^{-m^{\prime}}}{m^{\prime}!}\binom{K+m+1-\frac{d}{2}}{n}z^{m^{\prime}}W_{l,n}\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(f)({2K+2m+3-d})

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{m^{\prime}=0}^{K}\sum\limits_{\ \ l+n=K+m-m^{\prime}}\frac{4^{-m^{\prime}}}{m^{\prime}!}\binom{K+m+1-\frac{d}{2}}{n}W_{l,n}\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(f)({2K+2m+3-d})z^{m^{\prime}}.

We obtain

\displaystyle L[[2K+2m+3-d,m]]

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{l+n=K}\frac{4^{-m}}{m!}\binom{K+m+1-\frac{d}{2}}{n}W_{l,n}\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(f)({2K+2m+3-d})

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{n=0}^{K}\frac{4^{-m}}{m!}\binom{K+m+1-\frac{d}{2}}{n}\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(f)({2K+2m+3-d})W_{K-n,n}.\qed

For fixed
K
, the above equation has the general form of a system of linear equations

x_{m}=\sum\limits_{n=0}^{K}a_{mn}y_{n}.

Basic linear algebra tells us how to solve these equations for the
y_{n}=W_{K-n,n}
, given a left inverse for the matrix with entries
a_{mn}
. This left inverse is highly non-unique, as we are dealing with an “
\infty\times(K+1)
”-matrix (
m
can range over all of
{\mathbb{N}}
). We will proceed by computing the inverse of a
(K+1)\times(K+1)
block that starts at
m=o
for some offset
o
, in order to compute
W_{K,0}
. We will later obtain certain special cases by fixing
o
to some specific value.

Fixed Notation 5.2.4
.

For the remainder of this chapter, fix
o,K\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Set
\delta:=\frac{d}{2}-1-o
.

We will also need some efficient notation for a matrix or vector with entries specified by a function that cannot be easily written in an argument-free way. To achieve this, we reserve the letters
i
and
j
as placeholder variables for vector or matrix indices for the remainder of this chapter.

Definition 5.2.5
.

Let

\left[a(i,j)\right]

denote the
(K+1)\times(K+1)
matrix with
ij
-th entry
a(i,j)
(the indexing variables are set to be always
i
and
j
).
Let

\diag(b(i)):=\left[b(i)\delta_{ij}\right]

denote the diagonal matrix with entries
b(i)
and let

\left<b(i)\right>

denote the
K+1
-vector with
i
-th component
b(i)
. All indices are assumed to start at
0
(and thus run up to
K
).

An example calculation with this notation would be for any
a\in{\mathbb{R}}
:

\left[\binom{i}{j}\right]\left<a^{i}\right>=\left<\sum\limits_{l=0}^{K}\binom{i}{l}a^{l}\right>=\left<(a+1)^{i}\right>.

Lemma
5.2.3
can be expressed as a matrix-vector multiplication: Considering both sides as the
(m-o)
-th component of a vector and using the definition of
\delta
to slightly simplify notation, the statement of
5.2.3
for
o\leq m\leq o+K
is equivalent to

\left<L[[2K+2i+1-2\delta,i+o]]\right>=\left[\frac{4^{-(i+o)}}{(i+o)!}\binom{K+i-\delta}{j}\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(f)({2K+2i+1-2\delta})\right]\left<W_{K-i,i}\right>.

We thus aim to find a left inverse for the matrix

\displaystyle\left[\frac{4^{-(i+o)}}{(i+o)!}\binom{K+i-\delta}{j}\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(f)({2K+2i+1-2\delta})\right]

\displaystyle=\diag\left(\frac{4^{-(i+o)}}{(i+o)!}\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(f)({2K+2i-2\delta+1})\right)\left[\binom{K+i-\delta}{j}\right].

As the diagonal matrix is easy to invert as long as the Mellin transform of
f
is non-vanishing at the relevant integers, we may concentrate on finding an inverse to the second factor. We will do so in two steps, first reducing to another matrix of binomial coefficients, which we can then find an inverse to.

Lemma 5.2.6
.

We have

\left[(-1)^{i-j}\binom{i}{j}\right]\left[\binom{K+i-\delta}{j}\right]=\left[\binom{K-\delta}{j-i}\right].

Proof.

Define the (right) discrete derivative of a function
\phi
by

D_{m}\phi(m):=(D\phi)(m):=\phi(m+1)-\phi(m).

This acts on binomial coefficients as follows:

D_{m}\binom{a+m}{k}=\binom{a+m+1}{k}-\binom{a+m}{k}=\binom{a+m}{k-1}.

Applying this inductively, we obtain

\left.D_{i}^{l}\binom{K+i-\delta}{j}\right|_{i=0}=\binom{K-\delta}{j-l}.

We have with
I
denoting the identity and
A\phi(x):=\phi(x+1)
:

D^{l}\phi(0)=(A-I)^{l}\phi(0)=\sum\limits_{m=0}^{l}\binom{l}{m}(-I)^{l-m}A^{m}\phi(0)=\sum\limits_{m=0}^{l}(-1)^{l-m}\binom{l}{m}\phi(m).

Combining the two, we obtain

\displaystyle\left[(-1)^{i-j}\binom{i}{j}\right]\left[\binom{K+i-\delta}{j}\right]

\displaystyle=\left[\sum\limits_{m=0}^{K}(-1)^{i-m}\binom{i}{m}\binom{K+m-\delta}{j}\right]

\displaystyle=\left[\sum\limits_{m=0}^{i}(-1)^{i-m}\binom{i}{m}\binom{K+m-\delta}{j}\right]

\displaystyle=\left[\left.D_{m}^{i}\binom{K+m-\delta}{j}\right|_{m=0}\right]

\displaystyle=\left[\binom{K-\delta}{j-i}\right].\qed

We now proceed to find an inverse for the latter.

Lemma 5.2.7
.

\left[\binom{K-\delta}{j-i}\right]
is invertible with inverse

\left[\binom{\delta-K}{j-i}\right].

Proof.

With the Chou-Vandermonde identity

\sum\limits_{l=0}^{k}\binom{m}{k-l}\binom{n}{l}=\binom{m+n}{k},

(for
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
m,n\in{\mathbb{C}}
arbitrary), we obtain (taking sums to be zero if the upper bound is smaller than the lower bound of summation)

\displaystyle\left[\binom{\delta-K}{j-i}\right]\left[\binom{K-\delta}{j-i}\right]

\displaystyle=\left[\sum\limits_{l=0}^{K}\binom{\delta-K}{l-i}\binom{K-\delta}{j-l}\right]

\displaystyle=\left[\sum\limits_{l=i}^{j}\binom{\delta-K}{l-i}\binom{K-\delta}{j-l}\right]

\displaystyle=\left[\sum\limits_{l=0}^{j-i}\binom{\delta-K}{j-i-l}\binom{K-\delta}{l}\right]

\displaystyle=\left[\binom{0}{j-i}\right]

\displaystyle=[\delta_{ij}]

\displaystyle=1.\qed

We now put these results together. As we will have to divide by some values of
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(f)
, we have to assume that those are non-zero.

Fixed Notation 5.2.8
.

From now on, assume that
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(f)
is non-zero at integers.

Lemma 5.2.9
.

\left[\frac{4^{-(i+o)}}{(i+o)!}\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(f)({2K+2i-2\delta+1})\binom{K+i-\delta}{j}\right]

is invertible with inverse

\left[\sum\limits_{l=0}^{K}\frac{4^{j+o}(j+o)!(-1)^{l-j}}{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(f)({2K+2j-2\delta+1})}\binom{\delta-K}{l-i}\binom{l}{j}\right].

Proof.

Abbreviate

h(i):=\frac{4^{-(i+o)}}{(i+o)!}\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(f)({2K+2i-2\delta+1})

Using lemmas
5.2.6
and
5.2.7
, we obtain

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle=\left[\binom{\delta-K}{j-i}\right]\left[\binom{K-\delta}{j-i}\right]

\displaystyle=\left[\binom{\delta-K}{j-i}\right]\left[(-1)^{i-j}\binom{i}{j}\right]\left[\binom{K+i-\delta}{j}\right]

\displaystyle=\left[\sum\limits_{l=0}^{K}(-1)^{l-j}\binom{\delta-K}{l-i}\binom{l}{j}\right]\diag(h(i))^{-1}\left[h(i)\binom{K+i-\delta}{j}\right]

\displaystyle=\left[\sum\limits_{l=0}^{K}\frac{(-1)^{l-j}}{h(j)}\binom{\delta-K}{l-i}\binom{l}{j}\right]\left[h(i)\binom{K+i-\delta}{j}\right],

Thus

\left[\sum\limits_{l=0}^{K}\frac{(-1)^{l-j}}{h(j)}\binom{\delta-K}{l-i}\binom{l}{j}\right]

is the inverse for

\left[h(i)\binom{K+i-\delta}{j}\right].

Inserting the definition of
h
yields the desired result.
∎

With this, we can now obtain a formula for
W_{K,0}
(and thus the
K
-th Hadamard coefficient at the diagonal), yielding the abstractified version of our main result.

Theorem 5.2.10
.

We have

\displaystyle W_{K,0}=

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{m=0}^{K}\frac{4^{m+o}(m+o)!}{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(f)({2K+2m-2\delta+1})}\binom{\delta-K}{m}\binom{2K-\delta}{K-m}L[[2K+2m-2\delta+1,m+o]].

Proof.

The proof basically consists of combining lemmas
5.2.3
and
5.2.9
, with some extra calculations done in advance to simplify the result.
Using the identity

\binom{a}{b}\binom{b}{c}=\binom{a}{c}\binom{a-c}{b-c},

we get for any
l,m\leq K
:

\binom{\delta-K}{l}\binom{l}{m}=\binom{\delta-K}{m}\binom{\delta-K-m}{l-m}.

Using (for integer
b
)

\binom{-a}{b}=(-1)^{b}\binom{a+b-1}{b}

and

\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n}\binom{a+k}{k}=\binom{a+n+1}{n}

we get

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{l=0}^{K}(-1)^{l-m}\binom{\delta-K-m}{l-m}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{l=0}^{K}\binom{K-\delta+l-1}{l-m}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{l=m}^{K}\binom{K-\delta+l-1}{l-m}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{l=0}^{K-m}\binom{K-\delta+m-1+l}{l}

\displaystyle=\binom{K-\delta+m-1+K-m+1}{K-m}

\displaystyle=\binom{2K-\delta}{K-m}.

We obtain

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{l=0}^{K}(-1)^{l-m}\binom{\delta-K}{l}\binom{l}{m}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{l=0}^{K}(-1)^{l-m}\binom{\delta-K}{m}\binom{\delta-K-m}{l-m}

\displaystyle=\binom{\delta-K}{m}\binom{2K-\delta}{K-m}.

By viewing Lemma
5.2.3
as a vector identity, we have

\left<L[[2K+2i+1-2\delta,i+o]]\right>=\left[\frac{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(f)({2K+2i-2\delta+1})}{4^{i+o}(i+o)!}\binom{K+i-\delta}{j}\right]\left<W_{K-i,i}\right>.

By
5.2.9
and the previous calculation, we obtain

\displaystyle W_{K,0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left<W_{K-i,i}\right>_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\left[\frac{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(f)({2K+2i-2\delta+1})}{4^{i+o}(i+o)!}\binom{K+i-\delta}{j}\right]^{-1}\left<L[[2K+2i-2\delta+1,i+o]]\right>\right)_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\left[\sum\limits_{l=0}^{K}\frac{4^{j+o}(j+o)!(-1)^{l-j}}{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(f)({2K+2j-2\delta+1})}\binom{\delta-K}{l-i}\binom{l}{j}\right]\left<L[[2K+2i-2\delta+1,i+o]]\right>\right)_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{m,l=0}^{K}\frac{4^{m+o}(m+o)!(-1)^{l-m}}{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(f)({2K+2m-2\delta+1})}\binom{\delta-K}{l}\binom{l}{m}L[[2K+2m-2\delta+1,m+o]]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{m=0}^{K}\frac{4^{m+o}(m+o)!}{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(f)({2K+2m-2\delta+1})}\binom{\delta-K}{m}\binom{2K-\delta}{K-m}L[[2K+2m-2\delta+1,m+o]].\qed

The linear combination obtained above, including the extra factors hidden in the definition of
W
, will come up several times in the following results. We thus fix some notation for it.

Definition 5.2.11
.

For any function
\mathcal{L}
in two variables that has a suitable asymptotic expansion around
0
and
K,o\in{\mathbb{N}}
, define

\displaystyle\Xi_{K,o}(\mathcal{L}(s,z))_{s,z}:=\Xi_{K,o}(\mathcal{L}):=

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{m=0}^{K}\frac{\pi^{\frac{d}{2}-1}4^{K+m+o}(m+o)!K!}{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(f)({2K+2m+2o-d+3})}\binom{\frac{d}{2}-1-o-K}{m}

\displaystyle\cdot\binom{2K+o+1-\frac{d}{2}}{K-m}\mathcal{L}[[2K+2m+2o-d+3,m+o]].

We obtain our main formula for the Hadamard coefficients on the diagonal by replacing, in the abstract version above,
W
and
L
with the things they were created to resemble.

Theorem 5.2.12
.

Let
o,K\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Let
U\subset M
be GE,
I
an interval containing
0
,
w\colon I\rightarrow U
a timelike unit speed geodesic and
x=w(0)
. Let
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})
be odd and assume that
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(f)
is non-zero on
\mathbb{Z}
. Let
A:E\otimes E_{x}^{*}\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}
be an arbitrary bundle coordinate. Then we have

\displaystyle AV^{K,U}_{x}(x)=

\displaystyle\Xi_{K,o}(w^{*}(AG_{P-z,x})[f_{s}])_{s,z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{m=0}^{K}\frac{\pi^{\frac{d}{2}-1}4^{K+m+o}(m+o)!K!}{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(f)({2K+2m+2o-d+3})}\binom{\frac{d}{2}-1-o-K}{m}\binom{2K+o+1-\frac{d}{2}}{K-m}

\displaystyle\cdot w^{*}(AG_{P-z,x})[f_{s}][[s^{2K+2m+2o-d+3}z^{m+o}]].

Remark 5.2.13
.

In case
M
is not globally hyperbolic, we can still find an open, convex, globally hyperbolic negihborhood
U
for any point in
M
by
[
Min14
, Corollary 2]
(causal compatibility will be unobtainable if
M
is not strongly causal). We can then apply the theorem to
U
instead of
M
, as
U
is always causally compatible to itself. As the Hadamard coefficients on
U
only depend on
P|_{U}
(and
g|_{U}
), we still obtain a formula for the Hadamard coefficients on
U
. However, it will be in terms of the Green’s kernel for
P|_{U}
, which may not coincide with the restriction of the Green’s kernel for
P
(which need not even exist). In case
M
is strongly causal a GE neighborhood
U
does exist (
[
Min14
, Remark 14]
). For this,
[
BGP07
, Theorem 3.5.1]
implies that any Green’s operator for
P
restricts to the one for
P|_{U}
. Thus global hyperbolicity may be relaxed to strong causality if we are given a Green’s operator on
M
in advance.

Proof.

By Proposition
5.2.1
, setting

L(s,z):=w^{*}(A(G_{P-z,x}))[f_{s}]

and

W_{l,n}:=\frac{\pi^{\frac{2-d}{2}}n!}{4^{l}l!(2n)!}\left(AV^{l}_{x}\circ w\right)^{(2n)}(0)

satisfies the conditions assumed for this chapter. We may thus apply Theorem
5.2.10
(and bring the prefactor in
W_{K,0}
to the right hand side) to obtain the desired result.
∎

We can also integrate this expansion over any submanifold, using a cut-off in case it is non-compact:

Theorem 5.2.14
.

Let
N\subseteq M
be a submanifold and
\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(M)
. Let
A:E\boxtimes E^{*}\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}
be an arbitrary bundle coordinate and
A_{x}:=A|_{E\otimes E_{x}^{*}}
. Let
(w_{x})_{x\in N}
be a family of timelike unit speed geodesics defined on some interval
I
containing zero such that
w_{x}(0)=x
and
(x,t)\mapsto w_{x}(t)
is smooth. Then we have

\int\limits_{N}\chi(x)A_{x}V^{K,U}_{x}(x)dx=\Xi_{K,o}\Big(\int\limits_{N}\chi(x)w_{x}^{*}(A_{x}(G_{P-z,x}))[f_{s}]dx\Big)_{s,z},

with
\Xi
as defined in Definition
5.2.11
.

Proof.

Set

L(s,z):=\int\limits_{N}\chi(x)w_{x}^{*}(A_{x}(G_{P-z,x}))[f_{s}]dx

and

W_{l,n}:=\int\limits_{N}\chi(x)\frac{\pi^{\frac{2-d}{2}}n!}{4^{l}l!(2n)!}\left(A_{x}V^{l}_{x}\circ w_{x}\right)^{(2n)}(0)dx.

By Theorem
4.2.15
, this satisfies the assumptions for this chapter, so we may apply Theorem
5.2.10
which yields the desired result.
∎

The formula for the Hadamard coefficients can be simplified in various ways by choosing specific values for
o
for which
\Xi_{K,o}
takes a simpler form.

Theorem 5.2.15
.

Assume that
\mathcal{L}
is a function in two variables with an iterated asymptotic expansion. Let
K\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

1.

We have

\displaystyle\Xi_{K,0}(\mathcal{L})=

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{m=0}^{K}\frac{\pi^{\frac{d}{2}-1}4^{K+m}m!K!}{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(f)({2K+2m-d+3})}\binom{\frac{d}{2}-1-K}{m}\binom{2K+1-\frac{d}{2}}{K-m}

\displaystyle\cdot\mathcal{L}[[2K+2m-d+3,m]]

2.

If
d
is even, we have

\displaystyle\Xi_{K,\frac{d}{2}-1}(\mathcal{L})=

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{m=0}^{K}\frac{(4\pi)^{\frac{d}{2}-1}4^{K+m+\frac{d}{2}-1}(m+\frac{d}{2}-1)!K!}{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(f)({2K+2m+1})}\binom{-K}{m}\binom{2K}{K-m}

\displaystyle\cdot\mathcal{L}[[2K+2m+1,m+\tfrac{d}{2}-1]].

3.

If
d
is even and
K<\frac{d}{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\Xi_{K,\frac{d}{2}-1-K}(\mathcal{L})=

\displaystyle\frac{(4\pi)^{\frac{d}{2}-1}(\frac{d}{2}-1-K)!K!}{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(f)({1})}\mathcal{L}[[1,\tfrac{d}{2}-1-K]]

Remark 5.2.16
.

The third formula is just a re-creation of the special-case-formula we considered before.

Proof.

The first two statements follow immediately by inserting the appropriate value of
o
. For the last one, we use that
\binom{0}{m}=\delta_{m0}
. Thus all summands except
m=0
vanish and we obtain

\displaystyle\Xi_{K,\frac{d}{2}-1}(\mathcal{L})=

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{m=0}^{K}\frac{\pi^{\frac{d}{2}-1}4^{m+\frac{d}{2}-1}(m+\frac{d}{2}-1-K)!K!}{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(f)({2m+1})}\binom{0}{m}\binom{K}{K-m}

\displaystyle\cdot\mathcal{L}[[{2m+1},{m+\tfrac{d}{2}-1-K}]]

\displaystyle=\frac{(4\pi)^{\frac{d}{2}-1}(\frac{d}{2}-1-K)!K!}{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(f)({1})}\mathcal{L}[[1,\tfrac{d}{2}-1-K]].\qed

Instead of using Green’s operators for
P-z
, one can also use powers of the Green’s operators for
P
in all the formulas above:

Proposition 5.2.17
.

Under the conditions of Theorem
5.2.12
, we have for any
m,j\in{\mathbb{N}}
:

w^{*}(AG_{P-z,x})[f_{s}][[s^{2j+3-d}z^{m}]]=w^{*}(A(G_{x}^{+\ m+1}-G_{x}^{-\ m+1}))[f_{s}][[s^{2j+3-d}]].

Remark 5.2.18
.

Morally, the reason for this is that

G^{\pm}_{P-z}[[z^{m}]]=G^{\pm m+1}

in a way that is compatible with all the operations involved.

Proof.

By Theorem
3.1.9
, we have (using that
\binom{k}{n}
vanishes for
k>n
)

\displaystyle A(G_{x}^{+\ m+1}-G_{x}^{-\ m+1})

\displaystyle\sim\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\binom{m+k}{m}AV^{k,U}_{x}R^{U}(2k+2m+2,x)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\binom{k}{m}AV^{k-m,U}_{x}R^{U}(2k+2,x)

Note that
G^{\pm m}_{x}
is supported in the future/past of
x
, as
G^{\pm 0}_{x}=\delta_{x}
is supported at
x
and
G^{\pm m+1}_{x}=G^{\pm}G^{\pm m}_{x}
is supported in the future/past of
G^{\pm m}_{x}
.
We can thus use Proposition
4.2.12
to obtain

w^{*}(A(G_{x}^{+\ m+1}-G_{x}^{-\ m+1}))[f_{s}]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s\rightarrow 0}}{{\sim}}\sum\limits_{k,n=0}^{\infty}a(k,n)\binom{k}{m}(AV^{k-m}_{x}\circ w)^{(2n)}(0)s^{2k+2n+3-d},

for
a(k,n)
as in Definition
4.2.11
. We thus obtain

w^{*}(A(G_{x}^{+\ m+1}-G_{x}^{-\ m+1}))[f_{s}][[s^{2j+3-d}]]=\sum\limits_{k+n=j}a(k,n)\binom{k}{m}(AV^{k-m}_{x}\circ w)^{(2n)}(0).

On the other hand, as in Proposition
5.2.1
, we obtain from Theorem
4.2.14
and Proposition
3.2.4
:

w^{*}(AG_{P-z,x})[f_{s}]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s\rightarrow 0}}{{\sim}}\sum\limits_{k,n=0}^{\infty}a(k,n)\sum\limits_{m^{\prime}=0}^{k}\binom{k}{m^{\prime}}z^{m^{\prime}}(AV^{k-m^{\prime}}_{x}\circ w)^{(2n)}(0)s^{2k+2n+3-d}.

Thus (using again that
\binom{k}{m^{\prime}}
vanishes for
k>m^{\prime}
)

\displaystyle w^{*}(AG_{P-z,x})[f_{s}][[s^{2j+3-d}]]

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{n+k=j}\sum\limits_{m^{\prime}=0}^{k}a(k,n)\binom{k}{m^{\prime}}(AV^{k-m^{\prime}}_{x}\circ w)^{(2n)}(0)z^{m^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{m^{\prime}=0}^{j}\sum\limits_{n+k=j}a(k,n)\binom{k}{m^{\prime}}(AV^{k-m^{\prime}}_{x}\circ w)^{(2n)}(0)z^{m^{\prime}}

and hence

\displaystyle w^{*}(AG_{P-z,x})[f_{s}][[s^{2j+3-d}z^{m}]]

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{n+k=j}a(k,n)\binom{k}{m}(AV^{k-m}_{x}\circ w)^{(2n)}(0)

\displaystyle=w^{*}(A(G_{x}^{+\ m+1}-G_{x}^{-\ m+1}))[f_{s}][[s^{2j+3-d}]]\qed

Remark 5.2.19
.

One might wonder what would have happened if we had not required
f
to be odd, as this assumption was never really crucial to any part of the process.
Without this assumption, we would have instead obtained

w^{*}(AG_{P-z,x})[f_{s}]=L^{even}+L^{odd},

where
L^{even}
would be equal to the expression we obtained here for
f_{odd}
instead of
f
(i.e. with an asymptotic expansion involving even derivatives), while
L^{odd}
would be a similar expression depending on the Mellin transform of
f_{even}
and odd derivatives of the Hadamard coefficients instead. In the process of extracting the diagonal values,
L^{odd}
would have been useless, as it contains no immediate information about the value of
V_{K}
at the diagonal or its even derivatives. Thus in our inversion process, we would have had a completely independent matrix block to left-invert, which would not have influenced the inversion process for the first block. Overall, the end result we would have obtained for the (integrated) Hadamard coefficients would have been the same, except with
(f)_{odd}
instead of
f
.

Chapter 6
Global formulations

6.1
Global formulation in terms of Green’s operators

The formula for the Hadamard coefficients we have obtained thus far is still local in the sense that it depends on some geodesic
w
and the restriction of the kernel of
G^{\pm}_{P-z}
to that geodesic. This means it is not suitable for noncommutative settings, where geometric notions like geodesics no longer make sense a priori. In this chapter, we will investigate ways of making this at least partially global, so that it is defined only in terms of objects that could also exist on a noncommutative space. The latter condition is not really a rigorous one, as the notion of a Lorentzian noncommutative space is not yet fully formed.

Our overall goal is to find a formula for

\int\limits_{M}\chi(x)\tr(V^{k}_{x}(x))dx

where
\chi
is some cut-off function. This corresponds to the global heat coefficients in Riemannian geometry, apart from the cut-off, which we need to introduce as our manifold is non-compact. We want to express this integral in terms of the trace of some operator related to
G^{\pm}_{P-z}
, basically by integrating what we have done before over the diagonal.

We will still need to specify some direction as “time”, in order to have some timelines along which to integrate. In the local version, we chose a single curve
w
. For a global formula, we need to make this choice at every point, i.e. we need a family of curves
w_{x}
.

Fixed Notation 6.1.1
.

For the remainder of this chapter, let
(w_{x})_{x\in M}
be a family of timelike unit speed geodesics, defined on a intervals containing
0
, with
w_{x}(0)=x
such that
\bigcup\limits_{x\in M}\{x\}\times\Dom(w_{x})
is open in
M\times{\mathbb{R}}
and
(x,t)\mapsto w_{x}(t)
is a smooth submersion.
Let
\Phi
denote the induced flow in the first coordinate of
M\times M
, i.e.

\Dom(\Phi):=\{(y,x,t)\in M\times M\times{\mathbb{R}}|t\in\Dom(w_{y})\}

and

\displaystyle\Phi\colon\Dom(\Phi)

\displaystyle\rightarrow M\times M

\displaystyle(y,x,t)

\displaystyle\mapsto(w_{y}(t),x).

Remark 6.1.2
.

One example of this would be the flow curves of a timelike geodesic unit vector field. Vector fields also make sense as derivations on a noncommutative space, and, since connections also exist in that context, the condition of being geodesic can also be formulated there, so in this way one could probably define a special case of the above also in the noncommutative setting, though in general there is no guarantee that such a vector field exists. In case
d
is even and we only care about Hadamard coefficients for
k<\frac{d}{2}
, we may use the results of Section
5.1
instead of those of Section
5.2
and drop the condition that timelines are geodesics.

We have to work on
M\times M
, as we can no longer work with a fixed basepoint. We will need to define a lot of notation for the following propositions, mostly due to the fact that we cannot insert arguments into distributions and thus have to define everything in an argument-free way.

Fixed Notation 6.1.3
.

For
s>0
, define

\displaystyle F_{s}\colon\Dom(\Phi)

\displaystyle\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}

\displaystyle F_{s}(y,x,t)

\displaystyle:=f(\tfrac{t}{s}).

Let

\pi\colon\Dom(\Phi)\rightarrow M\times M

denote the canonical projection onto the first two factors and for
x\in M
,
let

\iota_{x}\colon M\rightarrow M\times M

denote the canonical inclusion defined by

\iota_{x}(y):=(y,x).

We shall sometimes need a cutoff to make sure we evaluate the flow only where it is defined: Fix
\chi_{\Phi}\in C^{\infty}(\Dom(\Phi))
that is constantly
1
in a neighborhood of
M\times M\times\{0\}
and vanishes near the boundary of
\Dom(\Phi)
in
M\times M\times{\mathbb{R}}
.

For the following considerations we need to use some knowledge about the wavefront of the causal propagator (see
[
Str09
, Theorem 16]
):

Theorem 6.1.4
.

Identifying
T^{*}(M\times M)
with
T^{*}M\times T^{*}M
, the wavefront of
\mathcal{K}(G)
only contains covectors of the form
(v,v^{\prime})
, where
v,v^{\prime}\in T^{*}M
are lightlike covectors.

This implies the well-definedness of certain operations for
\mathcal{K}(G)
. In particular, we can restrict to a point in the second coordinate. Unsurprisingly, we end up with the
G_{x}
we have used so far. We will need this to translate our previous results into the language of wavefront calculus.

Lemma 6.1.5
.

For any
x\in M
, and

\Lambda:=\{v\in\dot{T}^{*}M\mid v\text{ is lightlike}\},

the map

\iota_{x}^{*}\colon\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\WF(\mathcal{K}(G))}(E\boxtimes E)\rightarrow\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda}(E)

is continuous.
we have

G_{x}=\iota_{x}^{*}(\mathcal{K}(G)).

Proof.

Let
x\in M
. As we have

d\iota_{x}^{*}(v,v^{\prime})=v^{\prime},

\iota_{x}^{*}(\WF(\mathcal{K}(G)))
only contains lightlike covectors (and in particular no zero vectors). By wavefront calculus, this implies the first claim.
Let
L\in E_{x}
and
\psi\in\Gamma_{c}(E^{*})
.
For disambiguation we denote the Dirac distribution in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(E\otimes E_{x}^{*})
by
\delta_{x}
and that in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)
by
\delta_{x}^{0}
.
Let
(\delta_{n})
be a sequence of functions in
C_{c}^{\infty}(M)
supported in a fixed compact set
K
and converging to
\delta^{0}_{x}
in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)
. Let
(g_{n})
be a sequence in
\Gamma_{c}(E\boxtimes E^{*})
that converges to
\mathcal{K}(G)
in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\WF(\mathcal{K}(G))}(E\boxtimes E^{*})
. Let
B
be a basis of
E_{x}
containing
L
. For
b\in B
, choose a section
\hat{b}\in\Gamma_{c}(E)
with
\hat{b}(x)=b
and denote by
b^{*}
the element of the associated dual basis corresponding to
b
. The Dirac distribution takes the form

\delta_{x}=\sum\limits_{b\in B}\delta_{0}\hat{b}\otimes b^{*}.

With this, we can calculate

\displaystyle G_{x}[\psi\otimes L]

\displaystyle=G(\delta_{x})[\psi\otimes L]

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{b\in B}G(\delta^{0}_{x}\hat{b}\otimes b^{*})[\psi\otimes L]

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{b\in B}G(\delta^{0}_{x}\hat{b})[\psi]b^{*}(L)

\displaystyle=G(\delta^{0}_{x}\hat{L})[\psi]

\displaystyle=\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}G(\delta_{n}\hat{L})[\psi]

\displaystyle=\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathcal{K}(G)[\psi\otimes\delta_{n}\hat{L}]

\displaystyle=\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}(1\otimes\delta_{n})\mathcal{K}(G)[\psi\otimes\hat{L}]

The tensor product

C^{\infty}(M)\otimes\colon\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)\rightarrow\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\{0\}\times\dot{T}^{*}(M)}(M\times M)

is separately continuous and multiplication of distributions as a map

\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\{0\}\times\dot{T}^{*}(M)}(M\times M)\times\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\WF(\mathcal{K}(G))}(E\boxtimes E)\rightarrow\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(E\boxtimes E)

is separately continuous, as the wavefront of
\mathcal{K}(G)
contains no vectors that vanish in the first component.
This allows us to calculate

\displaystyle G_{x}[\psi\otimes L]

\displaystyle=\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}(1\otimes\delta_{n})\mathcal{K}(G)[\psi\otimes\hat{L}]

\displaystyle=(1\otimes\delta_{x}^{0})\mathcal{K}(G)[\psi\otimes\hat{L}]

\displaystyle=\lim\limits_{m\rightarrow\infty}(1\otimes\delta_{x}^{0})g_{m}[\psi\otimes\hat{L}]

\displaystyle=\lim\limits_{m\rightarrow\infty}\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}(1\otimes\delta_{n})g_{m}[\psi\otimes\hat{L}]

\displaystyle=\lim\limits_{m\rightarrow\infty}\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}\int\limits_{M}\delta_{n}(z)\int\limits_{M}g_{m}(y,z)(\psi(y)\otimes\hat{L}(z))dydz

\displaystyle=\lim\limits_{m\rightarrow\infty}\int\limits_{M}g_{m}(y,x)(\psi(y)\otimes\hat{L}(x))dy

\displaystyle=\lim\limits_{m\rightarrow\infty}\iota_{x}^{*}g_{m}[\psi\otimes L]

\displaystyle=\iota_{x}^{*}\mathcal{K}(G)[\psi\otimes L].

As every element of
\Gamma_{c}((E\otimes E_{x}^{*})^{*})=\Gamma_{c}(E^{*}\otimes E_{x})
is a finite linear combinations of simple tensors like
\psi\otimes L
, this implies the claim.
∎

In order to make sense of time translation in our vector bundles, we will use parallel transport to identify fibres at different times. Identifying them in any other way would yield mostly the same result.

Fixed Notation 6.1.6
.

For
t\in\Dom(w_{y})
, let
\Pi_{w}(y,t)
denote parallel transport from
E_{w_{y}(t)}
to
E_{y}
along
w_{y}
(with respect to the connection associated to
P
).
Define a bundle homomorphism

\Pi_{\Phi}\colon\Phi^{*}(E\boxtimes E^{*})\rightarrow\pi^{*}(E\boxtimes E^{*})

by setting, for
T\in\Phi^{*}(E\boxtimes E^{*})_{(y,x,t)}
,

\Pi_{\Phi}(T):=\Pi_{w}(y,t)\circ T.

For
\psi\in\Gamma(E)
and
t\in\Dom(w_{x})
, define time translation via

(\tau_{w}(t)\psi)(x):=\Pi_{w}(x,t)\psi(w_{x}(t)).

Let
A
be a bundle coordinate on
E\boxtimes E^{*}
such that for any
t\in\Dom(w_{x})
and
T\in E_{w_{x}(t)}\otimes E^{*}_{x}
we have

A(T)=\tr(\Pi_{w}(x,t)\circ T).

Let
A_{x}:=A\circ\iota_{x}
be its restriction to
E\boxtimes E_{x}^{*}
.

Remark 6.1.7
.

The bundle coordinate
A
is necessary due to the fact that
\tr(\Pi_{w}(x,t)\circ\cdot)
cannot be applied to a distribution in
E\boxtimes E^{*}
as it is only defined on a closed subset. However, eventually only the values on this subset will matter, so the way we extend is irrelevant. Such an extension can always be constructed in a smooth way by using a partition of unity.

We are interested in the quantity that is morally given by

L_{s}(x,y)=\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}f(\tfrac{t}{s})\Pi_{w}(y,t)G(w_{y}(t),x)dt,

for
s
sufficiently small. We particularly care about (morally)

tr(L_{s}(x,x))=\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}f(\tfrac{t}{s})\tr(\Pi_{w}(x,t)G(w_{x}(t),x))dt,

which we can relate to the trace of the Hadamard coefficients at the diagonal.
There are various ways to make this rigorous. In general, we need to include our cut-off
\chi_{\Phi}
to make sure this is well-defined for all
s
. At each individual point, however, if
s
is small enough the cut-off doesn’t impact the result. We need different ways to write the above in a rigorous fashion:

•

Writing

L_{s}(x,y)=\pi_{*}F_{s}\chi_{\Phi}\Pi_{\Phi}\Phi^{*}(\mathcal{K}(G))

will let us conclude by wavefront calculus that this is actually a smooth function.

•

Instead expressing the trace at the diagonal as

\tr(L_{s}(x,x))=w_{x}^{*}(A_{x}G_{x})[\chi_{\Phi}(x,x,\cdot)f_{s}]

will allow us to relate this to the Hadamard coefficients via the results of the previous chapter.

•

Finally, on compact sets and for
s
small enough such that the flow is well-defined without cut-off, the equality

L_{s}(x,y)=\mathcal{K}\left(\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}f(\tfrac{t}{s})\tau_{w}(t)Gdt\right)(x,y)

will allow us to relate the integral
\tr(L_{s}(x,x))
to the trace of that operator (both multiplied with suitable cut-offs to make the integral convergent and the operator trace class).

We proceed to show that all three ways of making
L_{s}
rigorous are well-defined and mutually compatible. The general strategy will always be the same: We use wavefront calculus to show that the expressions, as functions of
\mathcal{K}(G)
, are well-defined and continuous on distributions with wavefront set contained in that of
\mathcal{K}(G)
, then show that they are compatible on smooth functions to conclude that, by continuity, they also agree for
\mathcal{K}(G)
.

As a first step toward the first expression, we note that integration along timelines can be expressed as a pushforward by the corresponding projection:

Lemma 6.1.8
.

If F is a vector bundle on some manifold
N
,
U\subseteq N\times{\mathbb{R}}
is open,
p\colon U\times{\mathbb{R}}
denotes the projection onto the first component and
g\in\Gamma(p^{*}(E))
has compact support in each fibre of
p
, then we have

p_{*}g(x)=\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}g(x,t)dt.

Proof.

Both sides are smooth and for
\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(p(U))
we have

\displaystyle p_{*}g[\psi]

\displaystyle=g[\psi\circ p]

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{p(U)}\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}g(x,t)\psi(x)dtdx

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}g(\cdot,t)dt[\psi].

As
\psi
was arbitrary, this implies the claim.
∎

Thus, for a function
g
on
M\times M
, integration along flow lines can be written as

\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}f(\tfrac{t}{s})g(w_{y}(t),x)dt=\pi_{*}F_{s}\Phi^{*}(g)(y,x).

The right hand side also makes sense if
g
is a distribution with suitable wavefront set. This shows that our first expression for
L_{s}(x,y)
is well-defined.

Proposition 6.1.9
.

For any
s>0
,
g\mapsto\pi_{*}F_{s}\chi_{\Phi}\Pi_{\Phi}\Phi^{*}(g)
gives a well defined and continuous map from
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\WF(\mathcal{K}(G))}(E\boxtimes E^{*})
to
\Gamma(E\boxtimes E^{*})
.

Proof.

Multiplication with smooth functions and composition with bundle homomorphisms over the identity continuously map each wavefront space to itself. As
\Phi
is a submersion and
\pi
is proper on
\supp(\chi_{\Phi})
, wavefront calculus tells us that
\pi_{*}F_{s}\chi_{\Phi}\Pi_{\Phi}\Phi^{*}
is well defined and continuous as a map into
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\pi_{*}\Phi^{*}(\WF(\mathcal{K}(G)))}(E\boxtimes E^{*})
. We will show that
\pi_{*}\Phi^{*}(\WF(\mathcal{K}(G)))
is empty. Let
(y,x,t)\in\Dom(\Phi)
be arbitrary, let
\xi_{0}=(v,v^{\prime})
be in
\WF(\mathcal{K}(G))\cap T_{(w_{y}(t),x)}(M\times M)
and let
\partial_{t}
denote the the unit vector in
{\mathbb{R}}
-direction at
(y,x,t)
. Then we have

\displaystyle d\Phi_{(x,y,t)}^{*}(\xi_{0})(\partial_{t})

\displaystyle=\xi_{0}(d\Phi_{(y,x,t)}\partial_{t})

\displaystyle=\xi_{0}(\partial_{t}\Phi(y,x,t))

\displaystyle=\xi_{0}(\partial_{t}(w_{y}(t),x)

\displaystyle=\xi_{0}((w_{y}^{\prime}(t),0))

\displaystyle=(v(w_{y}^{\prime}(t)),0).

As
v
must be lightlike and
w_{y}^{\prime}
is timelike, this is non-zero by
2.2.3
. We can conclude that all covectors in
\Phi^{*}(\WF(\mathcal{K}(G)))
are non-zero when paired with
\partial_{t}
. For any
\xi\in T_{(x,y)}(M\times M)
, we have

d\pi_{(x,y,t)}^{*}\xi(\partial_{t})=\xi(\partial_{t}\pi(x,y,t))=\xi(0)=0.

Thus
d\pi_{(x,y,t)}^{*}\xi\notin\Phi^{*}(\WF(A\mathcal{K}(G)))
. As
\xi
was arbitrary,
\pi_{*}\Phi^{*}(\WF(A\mathcal{K}(G))
is empty and hence
\pi_{*}F_{s}\chi_{\Phi}\Pi_{\Phi}\Phi^{*}
maps
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\WF(\mathcal{K}(G))}(E\boxtimes E^{*})
into
\Gamma(E\boxtimes E^{*})
continuously.
∎

With this we can define

Definition 6.1.10
.

For
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
, define
L_{s}\in\Gamma(E\boxtimes E)
by

L_{s}:=\pi_{*}F_{s}\chi_{\Phi}\Pi_{\Phi}\Phi^{*}(\mathcal{K}(G)).

The above proposition implies that this is well-defined and smooth.

We now proceed to show that this coincides with the pointwise picture we have been using in the previous chapters:

Proposition 6.1.11
.

For arbitrary
x\in M
, set

\chi_{x}(t):=\chi_{\Phi}(x,x,t).

Then we have:

\tr(L_{s}(x,x)):=\tr\pi_{*}F_{s}\chi_{\Phi}\Pi_{\Phi}\Phi^{*}(\mathcal{K}(G))(x,x)=w_{x}^{*}(A_{x}G_{x})[\chi_{x}f_{s}].

Proof.

Let
(x,x,t)
be in
\Dom(\Phi)
. Let
\partial_{t}
be the standard unit vector in
T^{*}_{t}({\mathbb{R}})
. We calculate for lightlike
v\in T^{*}_{w_{x}(t)}(M)
:

\displaystyle dw_{x}^{*}(v)(\partial_{t})

\displaystyle=v(\partial_{t}w_{x}(t))

\displaystyle=v(w_{x}^{\prime}(t))

\displaystyle\neq 0,

as this is the pairing of a lightlike and a timelike vector. Thus
w_{x}^{*}(\Lambda)
, for
\Lambda
as in Proposition
6.1.5
, does not intersect the zero section. We can conclude that

w_{x}^{*}\colon\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda}(M)\rightarrow\mathcal{D}^{\prime}({\mathbb{R}})

is well-defined and continuous. Together with Proposition
6.1.5
, this means that all maps in the right hand side of the claim are continuous. The left hand side is continuous as a function of
\mathcal{K}(G)
by
6.1.9
, as evaluation is continuous on
\Gamma(E\boxtimes E^{*})
. We now calculate for
g\in\Gamma_{c}(E\boxtimes E^{*})
:

\displaystyle\tr\pi_{*}F_{s}\chi_{\Phi}\Pi_{\Phi}\Phi^{*}(g)(x,x)

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}f(\tfrac{t}{s})\chi_{\Phi}(x,x,t)\tr(\Pi_{w}(x,t)\circ g(w_{x}(t),x))dt

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}f_{s}(t)\chi_{x}(t)A_{x}(\iota_{x}^{*}g(w_{x}(t)))dt

\displaystyle=w_{y}^{*}(A_{x}\iota_{x}^{*}(g))[\chi_{x}f_{s}].

As both sides are continuous as functions of
g
in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\WF(\mathcal{K}(G))}(M\times M)
, the equality also holds for
\mathcal{K}(G)
instead of
g
. As
G_{x}=\iota_{x}^{*}\mathcal{K}(G)
, this finishes the proof.
∎

For the last expression, we need to establish continuity of operator evaluation in wavefront calculus.

Definition 6.1.12
.

For
\eta\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(F\boxtimes E^{*})
, denote by
\Op(\eta)
the unique continuous operator
\Gamma_{c}(E)\rightarrow\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(F)
with Schwartz kernel
\eta
.

Lemma 6.1.13
.

For
\psi\in\Gamma_{c}(E)
, the map
g\mapsto\Op(g)\psi
maps
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\WF(G)}(E\boxtimes E^{*})
to
\Gamma(E)
continuously.

Proof.

Let
p_{1}\colon M\times M\rightarrow M
be the projection onto the first component. For
g\in\Gamma_{c}(E\boxtimes E^{*})
and
\theta\in\Gamma_{c}(E^{*})
, we have

\displaystyle\Op(g)\psi[\theta]

\displaystyle=g[\theta\otimes\psi]

\displaystyle=(1\otimes\psi)g[\theta\otimes 1]

\displaystyle=(1\otimes\psi)g[\theta\circ p_{1}]

\displaystyle=p_{1*}((1\otimes\psi)g)[\theta].

As both sides are continuous as a function of
g\in D^{\prime}(E\boxtimes E^{*})
(multiplichation by
1\otimes\psi
ensures that the support condition for the pushforward theorem in wavefront calculus are satisfied), the equality holds for arbitrary distributions
g
.
In particular, we thus have for
g\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\WF(G)}(E\boxtimes E^{*})

\Op(g)\psi=p_{1*}((1\otimes\psi)g).

For
x,y\in M
and
v\in T_{x}M
, we have
(dp_{1})^{*}_{(x,y)}v=(v,0)
.
As
\WF(G)
does not contain vectors that vanish in the second component it does not intersect the range of
(dp_{1})^{*}_{(x,y)}
. Thus
p_{1*}(\WF(G))
is empty, and hence

g\mapsto p_{1*}((1\otimes\psi)g)

maps
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\WF(G)}(E\boxtimes E^{*})
to
\Gamma(E)
continuously.
∎

We now get to the last expression for
L_{s}
. In order to take integrals/traces later on, we need to multiply by cut-off functions on
M
. This also allows us to get rid of our flow-related cut-off
\chi_{\Phi}
, if we choose
s
small enough.

Definition 6.1.14
.

For a smooth function
\chi
, let
\mu_{\chi}
denote multiplication by
\chi
. For a family of Operators
(T(t))_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}
on some function space, define the integrated operator by

\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}T(t)dt\psi:=\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}T(t)\psi dt

with the integral taken pointwise (whenever this is defined).

Lemma 6.1.15
.

For
\chi_{1},\chi_{2}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(M)
, there is
s_{0}>0
such that for
s<s_{0}
, the operator

\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}\mu_{\chi_{1}}f(\tfrac{t}{s})\tau_{w}(t)G\mu_{\chi_{2}}dt

is well defined and has a smooth Schartz kernel given by

\mathcal{K}\left(\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}\mu_{\chi_{1}}f(\tfrac{t}{s})\tau_{w}(t)G\mu_{\chi_{2}}dt\right)=(\chi_{1}\otimes\chi_{2})L_{s}.

If
\Dom(\Phi)=M\times M\times{\mathbb{R}}
and
\chi_{\Phi}=1
this holds for arbitrary
s
and we may also allow
\chi_{1}=\chi_{2}=1
.

Proof.

As
\supp(\chi_{1})\times\supp(\chi_{2})
is compact and
\chi_{\Phi}^{-1}(1)
is a neighborhood of
M\times M\times\{0\}
, there is
s_{0}
such that

\supp(\chi_{1})\times\supp(\chi_{2})\times s_{0}I_{f}\subseteq\chi_{\Phi}^{-1}(1)\subseteq\Dom(\Phi).

Thus for
s<s_{0}
,

(\chi_{1}\otimes\chi_{2})\pi_{*}F_{s}\chi_{\Phi}(\cdot)=\pi_{*}(\chi_{1}\otimes\chi_{2}\otimes f_{s})(\cdot)

and
\chi_{1}\otimes f_{s}
has compact support contained in the domain of the flow, i.e. the region where
\tau_{w}(\cdot)G(\chi_{2}\psi)
is defined for any test function
\psi
. This means the operator under consideration is well-defined. If the flow is defined for all times and
\chi_{\Phi}=1
, we may instead consider arbitrary
s
.

We need to define quantities analogous to
\Phi
,
\pi
,
F_{s}
and
\Pi_{\Phi}
, only without the second argument.
Define

\phi(x,t):=w_{x}(t)

for all
(x,t)
where this is defined.
Let
\tilde{\pi}\colon\Dom(\phi)\rightarrow M
denote the projection on the first coordinate.
Finally, define

\Pi_{\phi}\colon\phi^{*}(E)\rightarrow\tilde{\pi}^{*}(E)

by setting for
v\in\phi^{*}(E)_{(x,t)}

\Pi_{\phi}(v):=\Pi_{w}(x,t)(v).

With this we can write rewrite our time translation (for
h\in\Gamma(E)
) as

\tau_{w}(t)(h)(x)=\Pi_{\phi}\phi^{*}(h)(x,t).

Using this and Lemma
6.1.8
, we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}\left(\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}\mu_{\chi_{1}}f(\tfrac{t}{s})\tau_{w}(t)\Op(g)\mu_{\chi_{2}}\right)(\psi_{1}\otimes\psi_{2})

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{M}\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}\chi_{1}(y)f_{s}(t)(\Pi_{\phi}\phi^{*}\Op(g)(\chi_{2}\psi_{2}))(y,t)dt\psi_{1}(y)dy

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{M}\tilde{\pi}_{*}((\chi_{1}\otimes f_{s})(\Pi_{\phi}\phi^{*}\Op(g)(\chi_{2}\psi_{2})))(y)\psi_{1}(y)dy

\displaystyle=(\tilde{\pi}_{*}((\chi_{1}\otimes f_{s})(\Pi_{\phi}\phi^{*}\Op(g)(\chi_{2}\psi_{2}))))[\psi_{1}].

By Lemma
6.1.13
,
\Op(\cdot)[\chi_{2}\phi_{2}]
maps
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\WF(G)}(E\boxtimes E^{*})
to
\Gamma(E)
continuously. All other maps involved are continuous maps on smooth sections (multiplication with
\chi_{1}\otimes f_{s}
guaranteeing that the support conditions for the pullback are met). Thus the above is continuous as a function of
g
.

Since

g\mapsto(\chi_{1}\otimes\chi_{2})\pi_{*}F_{s}\chi_{\Phi}\Pi_{\Phi}\Phi^{*}(g)

is also continuous, we only need to show the desired equality for
g\in\Gamma_{c}(E\boxtimes E^{*})
. In that case, we have for
\psi_{1}\in\Gamma_{c}(E^{*})
and
\psi_{2}\in\Gamma_{c}(E)
:

\displaystyle(\chi_{1}\otimes\chi_{2})L_{s}[\psi_{1}\otimes\psi_{2}]

\displaystyle=(\chi_{1}\otimes\chi_{2})\pi_{*}F_{s}\chi_{\Phi}\Pi_{\Phi}\Phi^{*}(g)[\psi_{1}\otimes\psi_{2}]

\displaystyle=\pi_{*}(\chi_{1}\otimes\chi_{2}\otimes f_{s})\Pi_{\Phi}\Phi^{*}(g)[\psi_{1}\otimes\psi_{2}]

\displaystyle=(\chi_{1}\otimes\chi_{2}\otimes f_{s})\Pi_{\Phi}(g\circ\Phi)[(\psi_{1}\otimes\psi_{2})\circ\pi]

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}\int\limits_{M}\int\limits_{M}f(\tfrac{t}{s})(\chi_{1}(y)\psi_{1}(y))(\Pi_{w}(y,t)g(w_{y}(t),x)(\chi_{2}(x)\psi_{2}(x))dxdydt

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{M}\psi_{1}(y)\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}f(\tfrac{t}{s})\chi_{1}(y)\Pi_{w}(y,t)\Op(g)(\chi_{2}\psi_{2})(w_{y}(t))dtdy

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{M}\psi_{1}(y)\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}f(\tfrac{t}{s})\chi_{1}(y)\tau_{w}(t)(\Op(g)(\chi_{2}\psi_{2}))(y)dtdy

\displaystyle=\mathcal{K}\left(\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}f(\tfrac{t}{s})\mu_{\chi_{1}}\tau_{w}(t)\Op(g)\mu_{\chi_{2}}dt\right)(\psi_{1}\otimes\psi_{2}).

By continuity, this also holds for
g=\mathcal{K}(G)
, proving the desired equality. Smoothness of the kernel then follows from Proposition
6.1.9
.
∎

We can now show the global version of our main formula (for readers who wish to understand this result without reading all the previous parts, we point out that there is a notation index at the end of the thesis):

Theorem 6.1.16
.

For
\chi_{1},\chi_{2}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(M)
, there is
s_{0}>0
, such that for
s<s_{0}
, the operator

\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}\mu_{\chi_{1}}f(\tfrac{t}{s})\tau_{w}(t)G\mu_{\chi_{2}}dt

extends continuously to a trace class operator in
L^{2}(E)
(with respect to any hermitian structure on
E
). Its trace has the asymptotic expansion

\displaystyle\tr\left(\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}\mu_{\chi_{1}}f(\tfrac{t}{s})\tau_{w}(t)G\mu_{\chi_{2}}dt\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s\rightarrow 0}}{{\sim}}

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}a(k,n)\int\limits_{M}\chi_{1}(x)\chi_{2}(x)\tr(\partial_{t}^{2n}|_{t=0}(\tau_{w}(t)V^{k}_{x})(x))dx\ s^{2k+2n+3-d},

for
a(k,n)
as in Definition
4.2.11
.

We have for any
k,o\in{\mathbb{N}}
:

\int\limits_{M}\chi_{1}(x)\chi_{2}(x)\tr(V^{k}_{x}(x))dx=\Xi_{k,o}\left(\tr\left(\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}\mu_{\chi_{1}}f(\tfrac{t}{s})\tau_{w}(t)G_{P-z}\mu_{\chi_{2}}dt\right)\right)_{s,z}

with
\Xi
as in Definition
5.2.11
.

Remark 6.1.17
.

The perfect analogue of the global heat coefficients would be

\frac{1}{k!}\int\limits_{M}\tr(V^{k}_{x}(x))dx.

However, as
M
is non-compact this generally does not exist and the best one can hope to obtain is an integral against some cut-off function
\chi
. Note that any cutoff can be written as a product
\chi=\chi_{1}\chi_{2}
.

Proof.

Choose
s_{0}
such that Lemma
6.1.15
applies and such that

\supp(\chi_{1})\times\supp(\chi_{1})\times s_{0}I_{f}\subseteq\chi_{\Phi}^{-1}(1).

In the following, assume
s<s_{0}
.

By Lemma
6.1.15
, the operator

\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}\mu_{\chi_{1}}f(\tfrac{t}{s})\tau_{w}(t)G\mu_{\chi_{2}}dt

has smooth compactly supported kernel. Thus it extends to a trace class operator on
L^{2}
-sections.

By Lemma
6.1.15
and Proposition
6.1.11
, using that
\chi_{\Phi}(x,x,t)f_{s}(t)=f_{s}(t)
for
x\in\supp(\chi_{1})
by our choice of
s_{0}
, we have for any
x\in M

\tr\left(\mathcal{K}\left(\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}\mu_{\chi_{1}}f(\tfrac{t}{s})\tau_{w}(t)G\mu_{\chi_{2}}dt\right)(x,x)\right)=\chi_{1}(x)\chi_{2}(x)w_{x}^{*}(A_{x}G_{x})[f_{s}]

Putting everything together and using Theorem
2.3.8
to express the trace of an operator as the integral over its kernel, we get

\displaystyle\tr\left(\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}\mu_{\chi_{1}}f(\tfrac{t}{s})\tau_{w}(t)G\mu_{\chi_{2}}dt\right)

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{M}\tr\left(\mathcal{K}\left(\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}\mu_{\chi_{1}}f(\tfrac{t}{s})\tau_{w}(t)G\mu_{\chi_{2}}dt\right)(x,x)\right)dx

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{M}\chi_{1}(x)\chi_{2}(x)w_{x}^{*}(A_{x}G_{x})[f_{s}]dx

using Theorem
4.2.15
, we obtain

\displaystyle\tr\left(\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}\mu_{\chi_{1}}f(\tfrac{t}{s})\tau_{w}(t)G\mu_{\chi_{2}}dt\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s\rightarrow 0}}{{\sim}}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}a(k,n)\int\limits_{M}\chi_{1}(x)\chi_{2}(x)\left(A_{x}V^{k,U}_{x}\circ w_{x}\right)^{(2n)}(0)dx\ s^{2k+2n+3-d}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}a(k,n)\int\limits_{M}\chi_{1}(x)\chi_{2}(x)\tr(\partial_{t}^{2n}|_{t=0}(\tau_{w}(t)V^{k}_{x})(x))dx\ s^{2k+2n+3-d}.

Applying Theorem
5.2.14
instead and using the previous equality for
P-z
instead of
P
, we obtain

\displaystyle\int\limits_{M}\chi_{1}(x)\chi_{2}(x)\tr(V^{k}_{x}(x))dx

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{M}\chi_{1}(x)\chi_{2}(x)A_{x}(V^{k}_{x}(x))dx

\displaystyle=\Xi_{k,o}\Big(\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}\chi_{1}(x)\chi_{2}(x)w_{x}^{*}(A_{x}(G_{P-z,x}))[f_{s}]dx\Big)_{s,z}

\displaystyle=\Xi_{k,o}\Big(\tr\Big(\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}\mu_{\chi_{1}}f(\tfrac{t}{s})\tau_{w}(t)G_{P-z}\mu_{\chi_{2}}dt\Big)dx\Big)_{s,z}.\qed

All the simplifications for special choices of
o
can be done in the global case just like in the local case and we obtain analogous formulas for the integrated scalar curvature.

6.2
Spacelike global formulation in terms of evolution operators

One approach to creating a notion of noncommutative spacetimes would be to make space noncommutative while keeping time to be
{\mathbb{R}}
. This is motivated by the fact that every globally hyperbolic manifold is diffeomorphic (but generally not isometric!) to
\Sigma\times{\mathbb{R}}
. Thus one simple notion of a noncommutative globally hyperbolic spacetime would be to take families of spectral triples
(A,\pi,D(t))
for
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, where the algebras, Hilbert spaces and representations are independent of
t
, while the Dirac operators depend on time in some ”smooth” way (to capture arbitrary globally hyperbolic spacetimes, one would also have to include a lapse function
{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow A
, but we will restrict to cases where this is one, as we need the timelines to be geodesics). In such a setting, it makes sense to talk about specific times, while all ”spacelike” quantities only make sense globally.

For this setting, we want to establish a version of our main theorem that is formulated in terms of evolution operators instead of Green’s operators, and where we integrate over space while keeping things local in time. Some versions of noncommutative Lorentzian geometry that work in a setting with commutative time use the evolution operators to identify different time-slices (see e.g.
[
Haw97
]
). Formulated in this framing, we get a formula in terms of the time shift with respect to the chosen timelines.

Fixed Notation 6.2.1
.

From now on assume that
M=\Sigma\times{\mathbb{R}}
such that all
\Sigma_{t}:=\Sigma\times\{t\}
are spacelike Cauchy hypersurfaces. Assume furthermore that
w_{(x,r)}(t)=(x,r+t)
are timelike unit speed geodesics and use the notation of the previous section for this family of timelines.

Remark 6.2.2
.

Not every globally hyperbolic spacetime can be brought to the form above. For example, every spacetime with finite time is excluded. If we assume furthermore that timelines are orthogonal to the
\Sigma_{t}
, which would be required to make sense of the evolution operators in terms of time derivatives, the condition that timelines are geodesics is equivalent to a constant lapse function.

Definition 6.2.3
.

Let
Q(t,r)
denote the evolution operator, as defined in Definition
2.6.2
.
Let
\Pi(r,t)
denote parrallel transport along timelines from
\Sigma_{t}
to
\Sigma_{r}
and for
\psi\in\Gamma(E|_{\Sigma_{t}})
, let

(\tau(r,t)\psi)(x,r):=\Pi(r,t)\psi(x,t).

We first formulate the Schwartz kernel we looked at before in terms of the evolution operator, by using the connection between the latter and the causal propagator (Proposition
2.6.5
).

Lemma 6.2.4
.

For any
r\in{\mathbb{R}}
, the operator

\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}f(\tfrac{t-r}{s})\tau(r,t)Q(t,r)dt

has Schwartz kernel given by

\mathcal{K}\Big(\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}f(\tfrac{t-r}{s})\tau(r,t)Q(t,r)dt\Big)=\mathcal{K}\Big(\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}f(\tfrac{t-r}{s})\tau_{w}(t-r)Gdt\Big)\Big|_{\Sigma_{r}\times\Sigma_{r}}.

Proof.

Let
\iota_{r}
denote the inclusion of
\Sigma_{r}
into
M
and let
\iota_{r,r}=\iota_{r}\times\iota_{r}
denote the inclusion of
\Sigma_{r}\times\Sigma_{r}
into
M\times M
.
Let
\psi\in\Gamma_{c}(E|_{\Sigma_{r}})
and
\rho\in\Gamma_{c}(E^{*}|_{\Sigma_{r}})
be arbitrary.
Abbreviate

K:=\mathcal{K}\Big(\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}f(\tfrac{t-r}{s})\tau_{w}(t-r)Gdt\Big).

As
K
is smooth (
6.1.15
), it may be paired with arbitrary distributions.
By Proposition
2.6.5
, we have

\displaystyle\rho\left[\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}f(\tfrac{t-r}{s})\tau(r,t)Q(t,r)\psi dt\right]

\displaystyle=\rho\left[\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}f(\tfrac{t-r}{s})\tau(r,t)(G(\iota_{r*}\psi)|_{\Sigma_{r}})dt\right]

\displaystyle=\rho\left[\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}f(\tfrac{t-r}{s})\tau_{w}(t-r)G(\iota_{r*}\psi)dt|_{\Sigma_{r}}\right]

\displaystyle=(\iota_{r*}\rho)\left[\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}f(\tfrac{t-r}{s})\tau_{w}(t-r)G(\iota_{r*}\psi)dt\right]

\displaystyle=K[(\iota_{r*}\rho)\otimes(\iota_{r*}\psi)]

\displaystyle=K[\iota_{r,r*}(\rho\otimes\psi)]

\displaystyle=\iota_{r,r*}(\rho\otimes\psi)[K]

\displaystyle=\rho\otimes\psi[K|_{\Sigma_{r}\times\Sigma_{r}}]

\displaystyle=K|_{\Sigma_{r}\times\Sigma_{r}}[\rho\otimes\psi]

Thus
K|_{\Sigma_{r}\times\Sigma_{r}}
is the Schwartz kernel of

\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}f(\tfrac{t-r}{s})\tau(r,t)Q(t,r)dt.\qed

We can use this to formulate our final result in terms of evolution operators instead of Green’s operators:

Theorem 6.2.5
.

For any
r\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
\chi_{1},\chi_{2}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Sigma_{r})
, the operator

\mu_{\chi_{1}}\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}f(\tfrac{t-r}{s})\tau(r,t)Q(t,r)dt\mu_{\chi_{2}}

is trace class and its trace has the asymptotic expansion

\displaystyle\tr\Big(\mu_{\chi_{1}}\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}f(\tfrac{t-r}{s})\tau(r,t)Q(t,r)dt\mu_{\chi_{2}}\Big)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s\rightarrow 0}}{{\sim}}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}a(k,n)\int\limits_{\Sigma_{r}}\chi_{1}(x)\chi_{2}(x)\tr(\partial_{t}^{2n}|_{t=0}(\tau_{w}(t)V^{k}_{x})(x))dx\ s^{2k+2n+3-d},

with
a(k,n)
as in Definition
4.2.11
.

Let
Q_{P-z}(t,r)
denote the evolution operator associated to
P-z
instead of
P
.
We have for any
k,o\in{\mathbb{N}}

\int\limits_{\Sigma_{r}}\chi_{1}(x)\chi_{2}(x)\tr(V^{k}_{x}(x))dx=\Xi_{k,o}\Big(\tr\Big(\mu_{\chi_{1}}\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}f(\tfrac{t-r}{s})\tau(r,t)Q_{P-z}(t,r)dt\mu_{\chi_{2}}\Big)\Big)_{s,z},

with
\Xi
as in Definition
5.2.11
.

Remark 6.2.6
.

If
\Sigma_{r}
is compact, one may omit the cutoffs
\chi_{1}
and
\chi_{2}
to make the result look a little simpler.

Proof.

By Lemma
6.2.4
and Lemma
6.1.15
, the operator has smooth Schwartz kernel. Due to the cut-off functions, the kernel is also compactly supported, so the operator is trace class by
2.3.8
.
These Lemmas and Proposition
6.1.11
also yield

\displaystyle\tr\Big(\mu_{\chi_{1}}\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}f(\tfrac{t-r}{s})\tau(r,t)Q(t,r)dt\mu_{\chi_{2}}\Big)

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{\Sigma_{r}}\chi_{1}(x)\chi_{2}(x)\mathcal{K}\Big(\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}f(\tfrac{t-r}{s})\tau_{w}(t-r)Gdt\Big)(x,x)dx

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{\Sigma_{r}}\chi_{1}(x)\chi_{2}(x)w_{x}^{*}(A_{x}G_{x})[f_{s}]dx.

Thus by Theorem
4.2.15
, we have

\displaystyle\tr\Big(\mu_{\chi_{1}}\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}f(\tfrac{t-r}{s})\tau(r,t)Q(t,r)dt\mu_{\chi_{2}}\Big)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s\rightarrow 0}}{{\sim}}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}a(k,n)\int\limits_{\Sigma_{r}}\chi_{1}(x)\chi_{2}(x)(A_{x}V^{k}_{x}\circ w_{x})^{(2n)}(0)dx\ s^{2k+2n+3-d}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}a(k,n)\int\limits_{\Sigma_{r}}\chi_{1}(x)\chi_{2}(x)\tr(\partial_{t}^{2n}|_{t=0}(\tau_{w}(t)V^{k}_{x})(x))dx\ s^{2k+2n+3-d}.

Replacing
P
with
P-z
, we get

\displaystyle\tr\Big(\mu_{\chi_{1}}\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}f(\tfrac{t-r}{s})\tau(r,t)Q_{P-z}(t,r)dt\mu_{\chi_{2}}\Big)

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{\Sigma_{r}}\chi_{1}(x)\chi_{2}(x)w_{x}^{*}(A_{x}G_{P-z,x})[f_{s}]dx.

Inserting this into Theorem
5.2.14
yields

\displaystyle\int\limits_{\Sigma_{r}}\chi_{1}(x)\chi_{2}(x)\tr(V^{k}_{x}(x))dx

\displaystyle=\Xi_{k,o}\Big(\tr\Big(\mu_{\chi_{1}}\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}f(\tfrac{t-r}{s})\tau(r,t)Q_{P-z}(t,r)dt\mu_{\chi_{2}}\Big)\Big)_{s,z}.\qed

Appendix: wavefront calculus on manifolds

In this appendix, we want to prove Theorem
2.7.11
, i.e. show that the results of wavefront calculus on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
carry over to vector bundles over manifolds.

In the following we omit the restriction to chart domains when composing with charts, and automatically restrict compositions involving charts to the sets where they are defined (which may be empty).
We work in the setting of Theorem
2.7.11
, i.e. we have vector bundles
(E_{l})_{l\leq 3}
over a manifold
X
, a vector bundle
F
over a manifold
Y
and closed conic subsets
\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}\subseteq\dot{T}^{*}(X)
and
\Theta\subseteq\dot{T}^{*}(Y)
.

Before we proceed with the claims of Theorem
2.7.11
, we need to establish that it is sufficient to consider only wavefront norms for a fixed open cover of charts, with local coordinate frames:

Define
E_{4}:=f^{*}(F)
.
Choose charts
(\psi_{i})_{\char 16\relax\in I}
of
X
whose domains
(U_{i})_{i\in I}
form a locally finite cover of
X
such that all relevant vector bundles are trivial over all
U_{i}
and let
(\chi_{i})_{i\in I}
be a partition of unity subordinate to these. Let
(\psi^{\prime}_{i})_{i\in I^{\prime}}
,
(U^{\prime}_{i})_{i\in I^{\prime}}
and
(\chi^{\prime}_{i})_{i\in I^{\prime}}
be the same for
Y
. For each
i\in I
and
l\leq 4
, let
(A^{l}_{ij})_{j\leq\rk(E_{l})}
be a frame of
E_{l}^{*}|_{U_{i}}
, let
(A^{l*}_{ij})_{j\leq\rk(E_{l})}
denote the corresponding dual frame. Let
(B_{ij})_{j\leq\rk(F)}
be a frame for
F^{*}|_{U_{i}}
, with dual frame
(B^{*}_{ij})
. Elements in the frames of the dual bundle can be interpreted as bundle coordinates. For a section or distribution
\eta
in some
E_{l}
resp.
F
write

\eta_{ij}:=\psi_{i}^{-1*}(A^{l}_{ij}\eta|_{U_{i}})

resp.

\eta_{ij}:={\psi^{\prime}}_{i}^{-1*}(B_{ij}\eta|_{U^{\prime}_{i}}).

Lemma 6.2.7
.

1.

We have

\eta=\sum\limits_{i\in I,j\leq\rk(E_{1})}\chi_{i}(\psi_{i}^{*}\eta_{ij})A^{1*}_{ij}.

2.

The wavefront of
\eta
is the union of all sets

\psi_{i}^{*}(\WF(\eta_{ij})),

for
i\in I
and
j\leq\rk(E_{1})
.

3.

The seminorms of a distribution
\eta\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda}(E_{1})
are equivalent to those of its components
(\eta_{ij})
in the corresponding spaces
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\psi_{i}^{-1*}\Lambda}(\Ran(\psi_{i}))
. This means for every
i\in I
and
j\leq\rk(E_{1})
, the seminorms of
\eta_{ij}
are bounded in terms of finitely many (actually, one) seminorms of
\eta
and conversely each seminorm of
\eta
is bounded in terms of finitely many seminorms of finitely many
\eta_{ij}
. Thus the topology of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\Lambda}(E_{1})
is equivalently described by the seminorms
